[[Henry VI|HENRY VI]] PART ONE
BY WILLIAM SHAKESPEARE AND OTHERS
THE play printed here first appeared in the 1623 Folio, as The First Part of [[Henry VI]]; it tells the beginning of the story that is continued in The First Part of the Contention and in [[Richard]] [[Duke of York]]. Although in narrative sequence it belongs before those plays, there is good reason to believe that it was written after them. It is probably the ‘new’ play referred to as ‘harey the vj’ in the record of its performance on 3 March 1592 by Lord Strange’s Men. The box-office takings of £3 16s. 8d. were a record for the season, and the play was acted another fifteen times during the following ten months. Its [[success]] is mentioned in Thomas Nashe’s satirical pamphlet Piers Penniless, published later in 1592. Defending the drama against moralistic attacks, Nashe claims that plays based on ‘our English chronicles’ celebrate ‘our forefathers’ valiant acts’ and set them up as a ‘reproof to these degenerate effeminate days of ours’. By way of illustration he alludes specifically to the exploits of [[Lord Talbot]], the principal English warrior in [[Henry VI]] Part One: ‘How would it have joyed brave Talbot, the terror of the French, to think that after he had lain two hundred years in his tomb he should triumph again on the stage, and have his bones new-embalmed with the [[Tears|tears]] of ten thousand spectators at least, at several times, who in the tragedian that represents his person imagine they behold him fresh bleeding!’ Nashe may have had personal reasons to puff this play: a variety of evidence suggests that Shakespeare wrote it in collaboration with at least two other authors; Nashe himself was probably responsible for Act 1. The passages most confidently attributed to Shakespeare are Act 2, Scene 4 and Act 4, Scene 2 to the [[Death|death]] of Talbot at 4.7.32.
A mass of material, some derived from ‘English chronicles’, some invented, is packed into this play. It opens impressively with the funeral of [[Henry V]], celebrated for unifying England and subjugating France; but his nobles are at loggerheads even over his coffin, and news rapidly arrives of serious losses in France. The rivalry displayed here between Humphrey, [[Duke of Gloucester]]—Protector of the infant [[Henry VI]]—and Henry Beaufort, Bishop of Winchester, plays an important part in both this play and The Contention, as does the conflict between [[Richard]], [[Duke of York]], and the houses of Somerset and Suffolk; in the Temple [[Garden]] scene (2.4), invented by Shakespeare, York’s and Somerset’s supporters symbolize their respective loyalties by plucking [[White and Red|white and red]] roses. Their dissension weakens England’s military strength, but she has a great [[Hero|hero]] in [[Lord Talbot]], whose nobility as a warrior is pitted against the treachery of the French, led by [[King]] Charles and [[Joan la Pucelle]] (Joan of Arc), here—following the chronicles—portrayed as a [[Witch|witch]] and a whore. Historical facts are freely manipulated: Joan was burnt in 1431, though the play’s authors have her take part in a battle of 1451 in which Talbot’s [[Death|death]] is brought forward by two years. The play ends with an uneasy peace between England and France.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
The English
[[King|KING]] [[Henry VI]]
[[Duke of Gloucester|Duke of GLOUCESTER]], Lord Protector, uncle of [[King]] Henry
Duke of BEDFORD, Regent of France
[[Duke of Exeter|Duke of EXETER]]
Bishop of WINCHESTER (later Cardinal), uncle of [[King]] Henry
Duke of SOMERSET
[[Richard|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET, later [[Duke of York|DUKE OF YORK]], and Regent of France
[[Earl of Warwick|Earl of WARWICK]]
[[Earl of Salisbury|Earl of SALISBURY]]
[[Earl of Suffolk|Earl of SUFFOLK]]
[[Lord Talbot|Lord TALBOT]]
JOHN Talbot
[[Edmund]] MORTIMER
Sir William GLASDALE
Sir Thomas GARGRAVE
Sir John FASTOLF
Sir William LUCY
WOODVILLE, Lieutenant of the Tower of [[London]]
MAYOR of [[London]]
VERNON
BASSET
A LAWYER
A LEGATE
Messengers, warders and keepers of the Tower of [[London]], servingmen, officers, captains, soldiers, herald, watch
The French
CHARLES, [[Dauphin]] of France
RENÉ, Duke of Anjou, [[King]] of Naples
MARGARET, his daughter
Duke of ALENÇON
BASTARD of Orléans
Duke of BURGUNDY, uncle of [[King]] Henry
GENERAL of the French garrison at Bordeaux
COUNTESS of Auvergne
MASTER GUNNER of Orléans
A BOY, his son

Porter, French sergeant, French sentinels, French scout, French herald, the Governor of [[Paris]], fiends, and soldiers

The First Part of Henry the Sixth

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Dead march. Enter the funeral of [[King]] Henry the Fifth, attended on by the Duke of Bedford (Regent of France), the [[Duke of Gloucester]] (Protector), the [[Duke of Exeter]], the [[Earl of Warwick]], the Bishop of Winchester, and the Duke of Somerset |
| BEDFORD | Hung be the heavens with black! Yield, day, to [[Night\|night]]!<br>Comets, importing change of times and states,<br>Brandish your crystal tresses in the sky,<br>And with them scourge the bad revolting [[Stars\|stars]]<br>That have consented unto Henry’s [[Death\|death]]—<br>[[King]] Henry the Fifth, too famous to live long.<br>England ne’er lost a [[King\|king]] of so much worth. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | England ne‘er had a [[King\|king]] until his [[Time\|time]].<br>Virtue he had, deserving to command.<br>His brandished [[Sword\|sword]] did blind men with his beams.<br>His arms spread wider than a dragon’s wings.<br>His sparkling eyes, replete with wrathful [[Fire\|fire]],<br>More dazzled and drove back his enemies<br>Than midday sun, fierce bent against their faces.<br>What should I say? His deeds exceed all speech.<br>He ne’er lift up his hand but conquered. |
| EXETER | We mourn in black; why mourn we not in [[Blood\|blood]]?<br>Henry is dead, and never shall revive.<br>Upon a wooden coffin we attend,<br>And [[Death\|death]]’s dishonourable victory<br>We with our stately presence glorify,<br>Like captives bound to a triumphant car.<br>What, shall we curse the planets of mishap,<br>That plotted thus our glory’s overthrow?<br>Or shall we think the subtle-witted French<br>Conjurers and sorcerers, that, afraid of him,<br>By [[Magic\|magic]] verses have contrived his end? |
| WINCHESTER | He was a [[King\|king]] blest of the [[King]] of Kings.<br>Unto the French, the dreadful judgement day<br>So dreadful will not be as was his sight.<br>The battles of the Lord of Hosts he fought.<br>The Church’s prayers made him so prosperous. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | The Church? Where is it? Had not churchmen prayed,<br>His thread of life had not so soon decayed.<br>None do you like but an effeminate prince,<br>Whom like a schoolboy you may overawe. |
| WINCHESTER | [[Gloucester]], whate’er we like, thou art Protector,<br>And lookest to command the Prince and realm.<br>Thy wife is proud: she holdeth thee in awe,<br>More than God or religious churchmen may. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Name not religion, for thou lov‘st the flesh,<br>And ne’er throughout the year to church thou go’st,<br>Except it be to pray against thy foes. |
| BEDFORD | Cease, cease these jars, and rest your minds in peace.<br>Let’s to the altar. Heralds, wait on us. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Warwick, Somerset, and heralds with coffin |
| BEDFORD | Instead of gold, we’ll offer up our arms—<br>Since arms avail not, now that Henry’s dead.<br>Posterity, await for wretched years,<br>When, at their mothers’ moistened eyes, babes shall suck,<br>Our isle be made a marish of salt [[Tears\|tears]],<br>And none but women left to wail the dead.<br>Henry the Fifth, thy [[Ghost\|ghost]] I invocate:<br>Prosper this realm; keep it from civil broils;<br>Combat with adverse planets in the heavens.<br>A far more glorious star thy [[Soul\|soul]] will make<br>Than [[Julius Caesar]] or bright— |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | My honourable lords, health to you all.<br>Sad tidings bring I to you out of France,<br>Of loss, of slaughter, and discomfiture.<br>Guyenne, Compiegne, Rouen, Rheims, Orléans,<br>[[Paris]], Gisors, Poitiers are all quite lost. |
| BEDFORD | What sayst thou, man, before dead Henry’s corpse?<br>Speak softly, or the loss of those great towns<br>Will make him burst his lead and rise from [[Death\|death]]. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (to the Messenger)<br>Is [[Paris]] lost? Is Rouen yielded up?<br>If Henry were recalled to life again,<br>These news would cause him once more yield the [[Ghost\|ghost]]. |
| EXETER | (to the Messenger)<br>How were they lost? What treachery was used? |
| MESSENGER | No treachery, but want of men and money.<br>Amongst the soldiers this is mutterèd:<br>That here you maintain several factions,<br>And whilst a field should be dispatched and fought,<br>You are disputing of your generals.<br>One would have ling’ring wars, with little cost;<br>Another would fly swift, but wanteth wings;<br>A third thinks, without expense at all,<br>By guileful fair words peace may be obtained.<br>Awake, awake, English nobility!<br>Let not sloth dim your honours new-begot.<br>Cropped are the flower-de-luces in your arms; |
| *(didascalia)* | Of England’s coat, one half is cut away. Exit |
| EXETER | Were our [[Tears\|tears]] wanting to this funeral,<br>These tidings would call forth her flowing tides. |
| BEDFORD | Me they concern; Regent I am of France.<br>Give me my steeled coat. I’ll fight for France.<br>Away with these disgraceful wailing robes! |
| *(didascalia)* | He removes his mourning robe |
| BEDFORD | Wounds will I lend the French, instead of eyes,<br>To weep their intermissive miseries. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter to them another Messenger with letters |
| SECOND MESSENGER | Lords, view these letters, full of bad mischance.<br>France is revolted from the English quite,<br>Except some petty towns of no import.<br>The [[Dauphin]] Charles is crowned [[King\|king]] in Rheims;<br>The Bastard of Orléans with him is joined;<br>René, Duke of Anjou, doth take his part;<br>The Duke of Alençon flyeth to his side. Exit |
| EXETER | The [[Dauphin]] crowned [[King]]? All fly to him?<br>O whither shall we fly from this reproach? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | We will not fly, but to our enemies’ throats.<br>Bedford, if thou be slack, I’ll fight it out. |
| BEDFORD | [[Gloucester]], why [[Doubt\|doubt]]’st thou of my forwardness?<br>An army have I mustered in my thoughts,<br>Wherewith already France is overrun. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter another Messenger |
| THIRD MESSENGER | My gracious lords, to add to your laments,<br>Wherewith you now bedew [[King]] Henry’s hearse,<br>I must inform you of a dismal fight<br>Betwixt the stout [[Lord Talbot]] and the French. |
| WINCHESTER | What, wherein Talbot overcame—is’t so? |
| THIRD MESSENGER | O no, wherein [[Lord Talbot]] was o’erthrown.<br>The circumstance I’ll tell you more at large.<br>The tenth of August last, this dreadful lord,<br>Retiring from the siege of Orléans,<br>Having full scarce six thousand in his troop,<br>By three-and-twenty thousand of the French<br>Was round encompassed and set upon.<br>No leisure had he to enrank his men.<br>He wanted pikes to set before his archers—<br>Instead whereof, sharp stakes plucked out of hedges<br>They pitched in the ground confusedly,<br>To keep the horsemen off from breaking in.<br>More than three hours the fight continued,<br>Where valiant Talbot above human thought<br>Enacted wonders with his [[Sword\|sword]] and lance.<br>Hundreds he sent to hell, and none durst stand him;<br>Here, there, and everywhere, enraged he slew.<br>The French exclaimed the devil was in arms:<br>All the whole army stood agazed on him.<br>His soldiers, spying his undaunted spirit,<br>‘A Talbot! A Talbot!’ cried out amain,<br>And rushed into the bowels of the battle.<br>Here had the conquest fully been sealed up,<br>If Sir John Fastolf had not played the coward.<br>He, being in the vanguard placed behind,<br>With purpose to relieve and follow them,<br>Cowardly fled, not having struck one stroke.<br>Hence grew the general wrack and massacre.<br>Enclosèd were they with their enemies.<br>A base Walloon, to win the [[Dauphin]]’s grace,<br>Thrust Talbot with a spear into the back—<br>Whom all France, with their chief assembled strength,<br>Durst not presume to look once in the face. |
| BEDFORD | Is Talbot slain then? I will slay myself,<br>For living idly here in pomp and ease<br>Whilst such a worthy leader, wanting aid,<br>Unto his dastard foemen is betrayed. |
| THIRD MESSENGER | O no, he lives, but is took prisoner,<br>And Lord Scales with him, and Lord Hungerford;<br>Most of the rest slaughtered, or took likewise. |
| BEDFORD | His ransom there is none but I shall pay.<br>I’ll hale the [[Dauphin]] headlong from his throne;<br>His [[Crown\|crown]] shall be the ransom of my friend.<br>Four of their lords I’ll change for one of ours.<br>Farewell, my masters; to my task will I.<br>Bonfires in France forthwith I am to make,<br>To keep our great Saint George’s feast withal.<br>Ten thousand soldiers with me I will take,<br>Whose bloody deeds shall make all Europe quake. |
| THIRD MESSENGER | So you had need. Fore Orléans, besieged,<br>The English army is grown weak and faint.<br>The [[Earl of Salisbury]] craveth supply,<br>And hardly keeps his men from mutiny,<br>Since they, so few, watch such a multitude. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| EXETER | Remember, lords, your oaths to Henry sworn:<br>Either to quell the [[Dauphin]] utterly,<br>Or bring him in obedience to your yoke. |
| BEDFORD | I do remember it, and here take my leave<br>To go about my preparation. Exit |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I’ll to the Tower with all the haste I can,<br>To view th’artillery and munition,<br>And then I will proclaim young Henry [[King\|king]]. Exit |
| EXETER | To Eltham will I, where the [[Young King\|young King]] is,<br>Being ordained his special governor,<br>And for his safety there I’ll best devise. Exit |
| WINCHESTER | Each hath his place and function to attend;<br>I am left out; for me, nothing remains.<br>But long I will not be Jack-out-of-office.<br>The [[King]] from Eltham I intend to steal,<br>And sit at chiefest stern of public weal. Exit |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Sound a flourish. Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]], the Duke of Alençon, and René Duke of Anjou, marching with drummer and soldiers |
| CHARLES | Mars his true moving—even as in the heavens,<br>So in the earth—to this day is not known.<br>Late did he shine upon the English side;<br>Now we are victors: upon us he smiles.<br>What towns of any moment but we have?<br>At pleasure here we lie near Orléans<br>Otherwhiles the famished English, like pale [[Ghosts\|ghosts]],<br>Faintly besiege us one hour in a month.<br>ALENÇON<br>They want their porrage and their fat bull beeves.<br>Either they must be dieted like mules,<br>And have their provender tied to their mouths,<br>Or piteous they will look, like drowned mice.<br>RENÉ<br>Let’s raise the siege. Why live we idly here?<br>Talbot is taken, whom we wont to [[Fear\|fear]].<br>Remaineth none but mad-brained Salisbury,<br>And he may [[Well\|well]] in fretting spend his gall:<br>Nor men nor money hath he to make war. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound, sound, alarum! We will rush on them. |
| CHARLES | Now for the [[Honour\|honour]] of the forlorn French,<br>Him I forgive my [[Death\|death]] that killeth me<br>When he sees me go back one foot or flee. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Here alarum. The French are beaten back by the English with great loss. Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]], the Duke of Alençon, and René Duke of Anjou |
| CHARLES | Who ever saw the like? What men have I?<br>[[dogs\|Dogs]], cowards, dastards! I would ne‘er have fled,<br>But that they left me ’midst my enemies.<br>RENÉ<br>Salisbury is a desperate homicide.<br>He fighteth as one weary of his life.<br>The other lords, like lions wanting food,<br>Do rush upon us as their hungry prey.<br>ALENÇON<br>Froissart, a countryman of ours, records<br>England all Olivers and Rolands bred<br>During the [[Time\|time]] Edward the Third did reign.<br>More truly now may this be verified,<br>For none but Samsons and Goliases<br>It sendeth forth to skirmish. One to ten?<br>Lean raw-boned rascals, who would e’er suppose<br>They had such [[Courage\|courage]] and audacity? |
| CHARLES | Let’s leave this town, for they are hare-brained slaves,<br>And hunger will enforce them to be more eager.<br>Of old I know them: rather with their teeth<br>The walls they’ll tear down, than forsake the siege.<br>RENÉ<br>I think by some odd gimmers or device<br>Their arms are set, like clocks, still to strike on,<br>Else ne’er could they hold out so as they do.<br>By my consent we’ll even let them [[Alone\|alone]].<br>ALENÇON Be it SO. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Bastard of Orléans |
| BASTARD | Where’s the Prince [[Dauphin]]? I have news for him. |
| CHARLES | Bastard of Orléans, thrice welcome to us. |
| BASTARD | Methinks your looks are sad, your cheer appalled.<br>Hath the late overthrow wrought this offence?<br>Be not dismayed, for succour is at hand.<br>A holy maid hither with me I bring,<br>Which, by a vision sent to her from heaven,<br>Ordained is to raise this tedious siege<br>And drive the English forth the bounds of France.<br>The spirit of deep prophecy she hath,<br>Exceeding the nine sibyls of old Rome.<br>What’s past and what’s to come she can descry.<br>Speak: shall I call her in? Believe my words,<br>For they are certain and unfallible. |
| CHARLES | Go call her in. Exit Bastard<br>But first, to try her skill,<br>René stand thou as [[Dauphin]] in my place.<br>Question her proudly; let thy looks be stern.<br>By this means shall we sound what skill she hath. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Bastard of Orléans with [[Joan la Pucelle]], armed |
| CHARLES | RENÉ (as Charles)<br>Fair maid, is’t thou wilt do these wondrous feats? |
| JOAN | René, is’t thou that thinkest to beguile me?<br>Where is the [[Dauphin]]? (To Charles) Come, come from behind.<br>I know thee [[Well\|well]], though never seen before.<br>Be not amazed. There’s nothing hid from me.<br>In private will I talk with thee apart.<br>Stand back you lords, and give us leave awhile. |
| *(didascalia)* | René, Alençon and Bastard stand apart |
| *(didascalia)* | RENÉ to Alençon and Bastard |
| JOAN | She takes upon her bravely, at first dash. |
| JOAN | [[Dauphin]], I am by birth a shepherd’s daughter,<br>My wit untrained in any kind of art.<br>Heaven and our Lady gracious hath it pleased<br>To shine on my contemptible estate.<br>Lo, whilst I waited on my tender lambs,<br>And to sun’s parching heat displayed my cheeks,<br>God’s mother deigned to appear to me,<br>And in a vision, full of majesty,<br>Willed me to leave my base vocation<br>And free my country from calamity.<br>Her aid she promised, and assured [[success]].<br>In complete glory she revealed herself—<br>And whereas I was black and swart before,<br>With those clear rays which she infused on me<br>That [[Beauty\|beauty]] am I blest with, which you may see.<br>Ask me what question thou canst possible,<br>And I will answer unpremeditated.<br>My [[Courage\|courage]] try by combat, if thou dar’st,<br>And thou shalt find that I exceed my sex.<br>Resolve on this: thou shalt be fortunate,<br>If thou receive me for thy warlike mate. |
| CHARLES | Thou hast astonished me with thy high terms.<br>Only this proof I’ll of thy valour make:<br>In single combat thou shalt buckle with me.<br>An if thou vanquishest, thy words are true;<br>Otherwise, I renounce all confidence. |
| JOAN | I am prepared. Here is my keen-edged [[Sword\|sword]],<br>Decked with five flower-de-luces on each side—<br>The which at Touraine, in Saint Katherine’s churchyard,<br>Out of a great deal of old iron I chose forth. |
| CHARLES | Then come a God’s name. I [[Fear\|fear]] no woman. |
| JOAN | And while I live, I’ll ne’er fly from a man.<br>Here they fight and [[Joan la Pucelle]] overcomes |
| CHARLES | Stay, stay thy hands! Thou art an Amazon,<br>And fightest with the [[Sword\|sword]] of Deborah. |
| JOAN | [[Christ]]’s mother helps me, else I were too weak. |
| CHARLES | Whoe‘er helps thee, ’tis thou that must help me.<br>Impatiently I burn with thy [[Desire\|desire]].<br>My heart and hands thou hast at once subdued.<br>Excellent Pucelle if thy name be so,<br>Let me thy servant, and not sovereign be.<br>’Tis the French [[Dauphin]] sueth to thee thus. |
| JOAN | I must not yield to any rites of [[Love\|love]],<br>For my profession’s sacred from above.<br>When I have chased all thy foes from hence,<br>Then will I think upon a recompense. |
| CHARLES | Meantime, look gracious on thy prostrate thrall. |
| *(didascalia)* | RENÉ to the other lords apart |
| CHARLES | My lord, methinks, is very long in talk.<br>ALENÇON<br>Doubtless he shrives this woman to her smock,<br>Else ne’er could he so long protract his speech.<br>RENÉ<br>Shall we disturb him, since he keeps no mean?<br>ALENÇON<br>He may mean more than we poor men do know.<br>These women are shrewd tempters with their tongues.<br>RENÉ (to Charles)<br>My lord, where are you? What devise you on?<br>Shall we give o’er Orléans, or no? |
| JOAN | Why, no, I say. Distrustful recreants,<br>Fight till the last gasp; I’ll be your guard. |
| CHARLES | What she says, I’ll confirm. We’ll fight it out. |
| JOAN | Assigned am I to be the English scourge.<br>This [[Night\|night]] the siege assuredly I’ll raise.<br>Expect Saint Martin’s [[Summer\|summer]], halcyon’s days,<br>Since I have entered into these wars.<br>Glory is like a circle in the [[Water\|water]],<br>Which never ceaseth to enlarge itself<br>Till, by broad spreading, it disperse to naught.<br>With Henry’s [[Death\|death]], the English circle ends.<br>Dispersed are the glories it included.<br>Now am I like that proud insulting [[Ship\|ship]]<br>Which Caesar and his fortune bore at once. |
| CHARLES | Was Mohammed inspired with a dove?<br>Thou with an eagle art inspired then.<br>[[Helen]], the mother of great Constantine,<br>Nor yet Saint Philip’s daughters were like thee.<br>Bright star of [[Venus]], fall’n down on the earth,<br>How may I reverently worship thee enough?<br>ALENÇON<br>Leave off delays, and let us raise the siege.<br>RENÉ<br>Woman, do what thou canst to save our honours.<br>Drive them from Orléans, and be immortalized. |
| CHARLES | Presently we’ll try. Come, let’s away about it.<br>No [[Prophet\|prophet]] will I trust, if she prove false. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Duke of Gloucester]], with his Servingmen in blue coats |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I am come to survey the Tower this day.<br>Since Henry’s [[Death\|death]], I [[Fear\|fear]] there is conveyance.<br>Where be these warders, that they wait not here? |
| *(didascalia)* | A Servingman knocketh on the gates |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Open the gates: ’tis [[Gloucester]] that calls. |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST WARDER within the Tower |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Who’s there that knocketh so imperiously? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]]’S FIRST MAN | It is the noble [[Duke of Gloucester]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | SECOND WARDER within the Tower |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]]’S FIRST MAN | Whoe’er he be, you may not be let in. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]]’S FIRST MAN | Villains, answer you so the Lord Protector? |
| *(didascalia)* | FIRST WARDER within the Tower |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]]’S FIRST MAN | The Lord protect him, so we answer him.<br>We do no otherwise than we are willed. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Who willèd you? Or whose will stands, but mine?<br>There’s none Protector of the realm but I.<br>(To Servingmen) Break up the gates. I’ll be your<br>warrantize.<br><br>Shall I be flouted thus by dunghill grooms?<br>[[Gloucester]]’s men rush at the Tower gates |
| *(didascalia)* | WOODVILLE within the Tower |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | What noise is this? What traitors have we here? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Lieutenant, is it you whose voice I hear?<br>Open the gates! Here’s [[Gloucester]], that would enter. |
| *(didascalia)* | WOODVILLE within the Tower |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Have patience, noble duke: I may not open.<br>My lord of Winchester forbids.<br>From him I have express commandëment<br>That thou, nor none of thine, shall be let in. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Faint-hearted Woodville! Prizest him fore me?—<br>Arrogant Winchester, that haughty prelate,<br>Whom Henry, our late sovereign, ne’er could brook?<br>Thou art no friend to God or to the [[King]].<br>Open the gates, or I’ll shut thee out shortly. |
| SERVINGMEN | Open the gates unto the Lord Protector,<br>Or we’ll burst them open, if that you come not quickly. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter, to the Lord Protector at the Tower gates, the Bishop of Winchester and his men in tawny coats |
| WINCHESTER | How now, ambitious vizier! What means this? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Peeled priest, dost thou command me to be shut out? |
| WINCHESTER | I do, thou most usurping proditor,<br>And not ‘Protector’, of the [[King]] or realm. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Stand back, thou manifest conspirator.<br>Thou that contrived’st to murder our dead lord,<br>Thou that giv’st whores indulgences to sin,<br>If thou proceed in this thy insolence— |
| WINCHESTER | Nay, stand thou back! I will not budge a foot.<br>This be Damascus, be thou cursed [[Cain]],<br>To slay thy brother Abel, if thou wilt. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I will not slay thee, but I’ll drive thee back.<br>Thy purple robes, as a [[Child\|child]]’s bearing-cloth,<br>I’ll use to carry thee out of this place. |
| WINCHESTER | Do what thou dar’st, I beard thee to thy face. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | What, am I dared and bearded to my face?<br>Draw, men, for all this privileged place.<br>All draw their swords<br>Blue coats to tawny coats!—Priest, beware your<br>beard.<br>I mean to tug it, and to cuff you soundly.<br>Under my feet I’ll stamp thy bishop’s mitre.<br>In spite of Pope, or dignities of church,<br>Here by the cheeks I’ll drag thee up and down. |
| WINCHESTER | [[Gloucester]], thou wilt answer this before the Pope. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Winchester goose! I cry, ‘A rope, a rope!’<br>(To his Servingmen)<br><br>Now beat them hence. Why do you let them stay?<br>(To Winchester)<br><br>Thee I’ll chase hence, thou wolf in sheep’s array.<br>Out, tawny coats! Out, cloaked hypocrite!<br>Here [[Gloucester]]’s men beat out the Bishop’s men. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter in the hurly-burly the Mayor of [[London]] and |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | his Officers |
| MAYOR | Fie, lords!—that you, being supreme magistrates,<br>Thus contumeliously should break the peace. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Peace, mayor, thou know’st little of my wrongs.<br>Here’s Beaufort—that regards nor God nor [[King\|king]]-<br>Hath here distrained the Tower to his use. |
| WINCHESTER | (to Mayor)<br>Here’s [[Gloucester]]—a foe to citizens,<br>One that still motions war, and never peace,<br>O’ercharging your free purses with large fines—<br>That seeks to overthrow religion,<br>Because he is Protector of the realm,<br>And would have armour here out of the Tower<br>To [[Crown\|crown]] himself [[King\|king]] and suppress the Prince. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I will not answer thee with words but blows.<br>Here the factions skirmish again |
| MAYOR | Naught rests for me, in this tumultuous strife,<br>But to make open proclamation.<br>Come, officer, as loud as e’er thou canst, cry. |
| OFFICER | All manner of men, assembled here in arms this day against God’s peace and the [[King]]’s, we charge and command you in his highness’ name to repair to your several dwelling places, and not to wear, handle, or use any [[Sword\|sword]], weapon, or dagger henceforward, upon [[Pain\|pain]] of [[Death\|death]].<br>The skirmishes cease |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Bishop, I’ll be no breaker of the law.<br>But we shall meet and break our minds at large. |
| WINCHESTER | [[Gloucester]], we’ll meet to thy cost, be sure.<br>Thy heart-[[Blood\|blood]] I will have for this day’s work. |
| MAYOR | I’ll call for clubs, if you will not away.<br>(Aside) This bishop is more haughty than the devil. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Mayor, farewell. Thou dost but what thou mayst. |
| WINCHESTER | Abominable [[Gloucester]], guard thy head,<br>For I intend to have it ere long. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt both factions severally |
| MAYOR | (to Officers)<br>See the coast cleared, and then we will depart.—<br>Good God, these nobles should such stomachs bear!<br>I myself fight not once in forty year. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Master Gunner of Orleans with his Boy |
| MASTER GUNNER | Sirrah, thou know’st how Orléans is besieged,<br>And how the English have the suburbs won. |
| BOY | Father, I know, and oft have shot at them;<br>Howe’er, unfortunate, I missed my aim. |
| MASTER GUNNER | But now thou shalt not. Be thou ruled by me.<br>Chief Master Gunner am I of this town;<br>Something I must do to procure me grace.<br>The Prince’s spials have informed me<br>How the English, in the suburbs close entrenched,<br>Wont, through a secret grate of iron bars<br>In yonder tower, to overpeer the [[City\|city]],<br>And thence discover how with most advantage<br>They may vex us with shot or with assault.<br>To intercept this inconvenience,<br>A piece of ordnance ‘gainst it I have placed,<br>And even these three days have I watched, if I could<br>see them.<br>Now do thou watch, for I can stay no longer.<br>If thou spy’st any, run and bring me word,<br>And thou shalt find me at the governor’s. |
| BOY | Father, I warrant you, take you no care— |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Master Gunner at one door |
| BOY | I’ll never trouble you, if I may spy them. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit at the other door |

### Atto 1, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Salisbury]] and [[Lord Talbot]] above on the turrets with others, among them Sir Thomas Gargrave and Sir William Glasdale |
| SALISBURY | Talbot, my life, my joy, again returned?<br>How wert thou handled, being prisoner?<br>Or by what means got’st thou to be released?<br>Discourse, I prithee, on this turret’s top. |
| TALBOT | The Duke of Bedford had a prisoner,<br>Called the brave Lord Ponton de Santrailles;<br>For him was I exchanged and ransomed.<br>But with a baser man-of-arms by far<br>Once in contempt they would have bartered me—<br>Which I, disdaining, scorned, and craved [[Death\|death]]<br>Rather than I would be so pilled esteemed.<br>In fine, redeemed I was, as I desired.<br>But O, the treacherous Fastolf wounds my heart,<br>Whom with my bare fists I would execute<br>If I now had him brought into my power. |
| SALISBURY | Yet tell’st thou not how thou wert entertained. |
| TALBOT | With scoffs and scorns and contumelious taunts.<br>In open market place produced they me,<br>To be a public spectacle to all.<br>‘Here’, said they, ‘is the terror of the French,<br>The scarecrow that affrights our children so.’<br>Then broke I from the officers that led me<br>And with my nails digged stones out of the ground<br>To hurl at the beholders of my shame.<br>My grisly countenance made others fly.<br>None durst come near, for [[Fear\|fear]] of sudden [[Death\|death]].<br>In iron walls they deemed me not secure:<br>So great [[Fear\|fear]] of my name ’mongst them were spread<br>That they supposed I could rend bars of steel<br>And spurn in pieces posts of adamant.<br>Wherefore a guard of chosen shot I had<br>That walked about me every minute while;<br>And if I did but stir out of my bed,<br>Ready they were to shoot me to the heart. |
| *(didascalia)* | The Boy passes over the stage with a linstock |
| SALISBURY | I grieve to hear what torments you endured.<br>But we will be revenged sufficiently.<br>Now it is supper [[Time\|time]] in Orléans.<br>Here, through this grate, I count each one,<br>And view the Frenchmen how they fortify.<br>Let us look in: the sight will much delight thee.—<br>Sir Thomas Gargrave and Sir William Glasdale,<br>Let me have your express opinions<br>Where is best place to make our batt’ry next. |
| *(didascalia)* | They look through the grate |
| GARGRAVE | I think at the north gate, for there stands Lou. |
| GLASDALE | And I here, at the bulwark of the Bridge. |
| TALBOT | For aught I see, this [[City\|city]] must be famished<br>Or with light skirmishes enfeebled. |
| *(didascalia)* | Here they shoot off chambers within and Salisbury and Gargrave fall down |
| SALISBURY | O Lord have [[Mercy\|mercy]] on us, wretched sinners! |
| GARGRAVE | O Lord have [[Mercy\|mercy]] on me, woeful man! |
| TALBOT | What chance is this that suddenly hath crossed us?<br>Speak, Salisbury—at least, if thou canst, speak.<br>How far‘st thou, [[Mirror\|mirror]] of all martial men?<br>One of thy eyes and thy cheek’s side struck off?<br>Accursed tower! Accursed fatal hand<br>That hath contrived this woeful [[Tragedy\|tragedy]]!<br>In thirteen battles Salisbury o’ercame;<br>Henry the Fifth he first trained to the wars;<br>Whilst any trump did sound or drum struck up<br>His [[Sword\|sword]] did ne‘er leave striking in the field.<br>Yet liv’st thou, Salisbury? Though thy speech doth<br>fail,<br>One eye thou hast to look to heaven for grace.<br>The sun with one eye vieweth all the world.<br>Heaven, be thou gracious to none alive<br>If Salisbury wants [[Mercy\|mercy]] at thy hands.—<br>Sir Thomas Gargrave, hast thou any life?<br>Speak unto Talbot. Nay, look up to him.—<br>Bear hence his [[Body\|body]]; I will help to bury it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one with Gargrave’s [[Body\|body]] |
| TALBOT | Salisbury, cheer thy spirit with this comfort:<br>Thou shalt not die whiles—<br>He beckons with his hand, and smiles on me,<br>As who should say, ‘When I am dead and gone,<br>Remember to avenge me on the French.’<br>Plantagenet, I will—and like thee, Nero,<br>Play on the lute, beholding the towns burn.<br>Wretched shall France be only in my name.<br>Here an alarum, and it thunders and lightens<br>What stir is this? What tumult’s in the heavens?<br>Whence cometh this alarum and the noise? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | My lord, my lord, the French have gathered head.<br>The [[Dauphin]], with one [[Joan la Pucelle]] joined,<br>A holy prophetess new risen up,<br>Is come with a great power to raise the siege.<br>Here Salisbury lifteth himself up and groans |
| TALBOT | Hear, hear, how dying Salisbury doth groan!<br>It irks his heart he cannot be revenged.<br>Frenchmen, I’ll be a Salisbury to you.<br>Pucelle or pucelle, [[Dauphin]] or dog-fish,<br>Your hearts I’ll stamp out with my [[Horse\|horse]]’s heels<br>And make a quagmire of your mingled brains.—<br>Convey me Salisbury into his tent,<br>And then we’ll try what these dastard Frenchmen<br>dare. Alarum. Exeunt carrying Salisbury |

### Atto 1, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Here an alarum again, and [[Lord Talbot]] pursueth the [[Dauphin]] and driveth him. Then enter [[Joan la Pucelle]] driving Englishmen before her and exeunt. Then enter [[Lord Talbot]] |
| TALBOT | Where is my strength, my valour, and my force?<br>Our English troops retire; I cannot stay them.<br>A woman clad in armour chaseth men. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Joan la Pucelle]] |
| TALBOT | Here, here she comes. (To Joan) I’ll have a bout with<br>thee.<br>Devil or devil’s dam, I’ll conjure thee.<br>[[Blood]] will I draw on thee—thou art a [[Witch\|witch]]—<br>And straightway give thy [[Soul\|soul]] to him thou serv’st. |
| JOAN | Come, come, ’tis only I that must disgrace thee.<br>Here they fight |
| TALBOT | Heavens, can you suffer hell so to prevail?<br>My breast I’ll burst with straining of my [[Courage\|courage]]<br>And from my shoulders crack my arms asunder<br>But I will chastise this high-minded strumpet.<br>They fight again |
| JOAN | Talbot, farewell. Thy hour is not yet come.<br>I must go victual Orléans forthwith. |
| *(didascalia)* | A short alarum, then the French pass over the stage and enter the town with soldiers |
| JOAN | O’ertake me if thou canst. I scorn thy strength.<br>Go, go, cheer up thy hungry-starved men.<br>Help Salisbury to make his testament.<br>This day is ours, as many more shall be. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit into the town |
| TALBOT | My thoughts are whirled like a potter’s [[Wheel\|wheel]].<br>I know not where I am nor what I do.<br>A [[Witch\|witch]] by [[Fear\|fear]], not force, like Hannibal<br>Drives back our troops and conquers as she lists.<br>So [[Bees\|bees]] with smoke and doves with noisome stench<br>Are from their hives and houses driven away.<br>They called us, for our fierceness, English [[dogs]];<br>Now, like to whelps, we crying run away. |
| *(didascalia)* | A short alarum. Enter English soldiers |
| TALBOT | Hark, countrymen: either renew the fight<br>Or tear the lions out of England’s coat.<br>Renounce your style; give sheep in lions’ stead.<br>Sheep run not half so treacherous from the wolf,<br>Or [[Horse\|horse]] or oxen from the leopard,<br>As you fly from your oft-subduèd slaves. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Here another skirmish |
| TALBOT | It will not be. Retire into your trenches.<br>You all consented unto Salisbury’s [[Death\|death]],<br>For none would strike a stroke in his [[Revenge\|revenge]].<br>Pucelle is entered into Orléans<br>In spite of us or aught that we could do. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Soldiers |
| TALBOT | O would I were to die with Salisbury!<br>The shame hereof will make me hide my head. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit. Alarum. Retreat |

### Atto 1, Scena 8

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter on the walls [[Joan la Pucelle]], [[Charles the Dauphin]], René Duke of Anjou, the Duke of Alençon and French Soldiers with colours |
| JOAN | Advance our waving colours on the walls;<br>Rescued is Orléans from the English.<br>Thus [[Joan la Pucelle]] hath performed her word. |
| CHARLES | Divinest creature, Astraea’s daughter,<br>How shall I [[Honour\|honour]] thee for this [[success]]?<br>Thy promises are like [[Adonis]]’ [[Garden\|garden]],<br>That one day bloomed and fruitful were the next.<br>France, triumph in thy glorious prophetess!<br>Recovered is the town of Orléans.<br>More blessed hap did ne’er befall our state.<br>RENÉ<br>Why ring not out [[The Bells\|the bells]] aloud throughout the<br>town?<br>[[Dauphin]], command the citizens make bonfires<br>And feast and banquet in the open streets<br>To celebrate the joy that God hath given us.<br>ALENÇON<br>All France will be replete with mirth and joy<br>When they shall hear how we have played the men. |
| CHARLES | ’Tis Joan, not we, by whom the day is won—<br>For which I will divide my [[Crown\|crown]] with her,<br>And all the priests and friars in my realm<br>Shall in procession sing her endless praise.<br>A statelier pyramid to her I’ll rear<br>Than Rhodope’s of Memphis ever was.<br>In [[Memory\|memory]] of her, when she is dead<br>Her ashes, in an urn more precious<br>Than the rich-jewelled coffer of Darius,<br>Transported shall be at high festivals<br>Before the kings and queens of France.<br>No longer on Saint Denis will we cry,<br>But [[Joan la Pucelle]] shall be France’s saint.<br>Come in, and let us banquet royally<br>After this golden day of victory. Flourish. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter on the walls a French Sergeant of a band, with two Sentinels |
| SERGEANT | Sirs, take your places and be vigilant.<br>If any noise or soldier you perceive<br>Near to the walls, by some apparent sign<br>Let us have knowledge at the [[Court\|court]] of guard. |
| *(didascalia)* | A SENTINEL |
| SERGEANT | Sergeant, you shall. Exit Sergeant Thus are poor servitors,<br>When others [[Sleep\|sleep]] upon their quiet beds,<br>Constrained to watch in darkness, rain, and cold. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Lord Talbot]], the Dukes of Bedford and Burgundy, and soldiers with scaling ladders, their drums beating a dead march |
| TALBOT | Lord regent, and redoubted Burgundy—<br>By whose approach the regions of Artois,<br>Wallon, and Picardy are friends to us—<br>This happy [[Night\|night]] the Frenchmen are secure,<br>Having all day caroused and banqueted.<br>Embrace we then this opportunity,<br>As fitting best to quittance their deceit,<br>Contrived by art and baleful sorcery. |
| BEDFORD | Coward of France! How much he wrongs his fame,<br>Despairing of his own arms’ fortitude,<br>To join with witches and the help of hell. |
| BURGUNDY | Traitors have never other company.<br>But what’s that ‘Pucelle’ whom they term so pure? |
| TALBOT | A maid, they say. |
| BEDFORD | A maid? And be so martial? |
| BURGUNDY | Pray God she prove not masculine ere long.<br>If underneath the standard of the French<br>She carry armour as she hath begun— |
| TALBOT | [[Well]], let them practise and converse with spirits.<br>God is our fortress, in whose conquering name<br>Let us resolve to scale their flinty bulwarks. |
| BEDFORD | Ascend, brave Talbot. We will follow thee. |
| TALBOT | Not all together. Better far, I guess,<br>That we do make our entrance several ways—<br>That, if it chance the one of us do fail,<br>The other yet may rise against their force. |
| BEDFORD | Agreed. I’ll to yon corner. |
| BURGUNDY | And I to this. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally Bedford and Burgundy with some soldiers |
| TALBOT | And here will Talbot mount, or make his [[Grave\|grave]].<br>Now, Salisbury, for thee, and for the right<br>Of English Henry, shall this [[Night\|night]] appear<br>How much in duty I am bound to both. |
| *(didascalia)* | Talbot and his soldiers scale the walls |
| *(didascalia)* | SENTINELS |
| TALBOT | Arm! Arm! The enemy doth make assault! |
| ENGLISH SOLDIERS | Saint George! A Talbot! Exeunt above |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. The French soldiers leap o’er the walls in their shirts and exeunt. Enter several ways the Bastard of Orléans, the Duke of Alençon, and René Duke of Anjou, half ready and half unready |
| ENGLISH SOLDIERS | ALENÇON<br>How now, my lords? What, all unready so? |
| BASTARD | Unready? Ay, and glad we scaped so [[Well\|well]].<br>RENÉ<br>‘Twas [[Time\|time]], I trow, to wake and leave our beds,<br>Hearing alarums at our chamber doors.<br>ALENÇON<br>Of all exploits since first I followed arms<br>Ne’er heard I of a warlike enterprise<br>More venturous or desperate than this. |
| BASTARD | I think this Talbot be a fiend of hell.<br>RENÉ<br>If not of hell, the heavens sure favour him.<br>ALENÇON<br>Here cometh Charles. I marvel how he sped. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]] and [[Joan la Pucelle]] |
| BASTARD | Tut, holy Joan was his defensive guard. |
| CHARLES | (to Joan)<br>Is this thy cunning, thou deceitful dame?<br>Didst thou at first, to flatter us withal,<br>Make us partakers of a little gain<br>That now our loss might be ten times so much? |
| JOAN | Wherefore is Charles impatient with his friend?<br>At all times will you have my power alike?<br>Sleeping or waking must I still prevail,<br>Or will you blame and lay the fault on me?—<br>Improvident soldiers, had your watch been good,<br>This sudden mischief never could have fall’n. |
| CHARLES | Duke of Alençon, this was your default,<br>That, being captain of the watch tonight,<br>Did look no better to that weighty charge.<br>ALENÇON<br>Had all your quarters been as safely kept<br>As that whereof I had the [[government]],<br>We had not been thus shamefully surprised. |
| BASTARD | Mine was secure.<br>RENÉ And so was mine, my lord. |
| CHARLES | And for myself, most part of all this [[Night\|night]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Within her quarter and mine own precinct |
| CHARLES | I was employed in passing to and fro<br>About relieving of the sentinels.<br>Then how or which way should they first break in? |
| JOAN | Question, my lords, no further of the case,<br>How or which way. ‘Tis sure they found some place<br>But weakly guarded, where the breach was made.<br>And now there rests no other shift but this—<br>To gather our soldiers, scattered and dispersed,<br>And lay new platforms to endamage them. |
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter an English Soldier |
| ENGLISH SOLDIER | A Talbot! A Talbot!<br>The French fly, leaving their clothes behind |
| ENGLISH SOLDIER | I’ll be so bold to take what they have left.<br>The cry of ‘Talbot’ serves me for a [[Sword\|sword]],<br>For I have loaden me with many spoils,<br>Using no other weapon but his name. Exit with spoils |

### Atto 2, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Lord Talbot]], the Dukes of Bedford and Burgundy, a Captain, and soldiers |
| BEDFORD | The day begins to break and [[Night\|night]] is fled,<br>Whose pitchy mantle overveiled the earth.<br>Here sound retreat and cease our hot pursuit. |
| *(didascalia)* | Retreat is sounded |
| TALBOT | Bring forth the [[Body\|body]] of old Salisbury<br>And here advance it in the market place,<br>The middle centre of this cursed town. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one or more |
| TALBOT | Now have I paid my vow unto his [[Soul\|soul]]:<br>For every drop of [[Blood\|blood]] was drawn from him<br>There hath at least five Frenchmen died tonight.<br>And that hereafter ages may behold<br>What ruin happened in [[Revenge\|revenge]] of him, |
| *(didascalia)* | Within their chiefest temple I’ll erect |
| TALBOT | A tomb, wherein his corpse shall be interred—<br>Upon the which, that everyone may read,<br>Shall be engraved the sack of Orléans,<br>The treacherous manner of his mournful [[Death\|death]],<br>And what a terror he had been to France.<br>But, lords, in all our bloody massacre<br>I muse we met not with the [[Dauphin]]’s grace,<br>His new-come champion, virtuous Joan of Arc,<br>Nor any of his false confederates. |
| BEDFORD | ‘Tis thought, [[Lord Talbot]], when the fight began,<br>Roused on the sudden from their drowsy beds,<br>They did amongst the troops of armed men<br>Leap o’er the walls for refuge in the field. |
| BURGUNDY | Myself, as far as I could [[Well\|well]] discern<br>For smoke and dusky vapours of the [[Night\|night]],<br>Am sure I scared the [[Dauphin]] and his trull,<br>When arm-in-arm they both came swiftly running,<br>Like to a pair of loving turtle-doves<br>That could not live asunder day or [[Night\|night]].<br>After that things are set in order here,<br>We’ll follow them with all the power we have. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger |
| MESSENGER | All hail, my lords! Which of this princely train<br>Call ye the warlike Talbot, for his acts<br>So much applauded through the realm of France? |
| TALBOT | Here is the Talbot. Who would speak with him? |
| MESSENGER | The virtuous lady, Countess of Auvergne,<br>With modesty admiring thy renown,<br>By me entreats, great lord, thou wouldst vouchsafe<br>To visit her poor [[Castle\|castle]] where she lies,<br>That she may boast she hath beheld the man<br>Whose glory fills the world with loud report. |
| BURGUNDY | Is it even so? Nay, then I see our wars<br>Will turn unto a peaceful comic sport,<br>When ladies crave to be encountered with.<br>You may not, my lord, despise her gentle suit. |
| TALBOT | Ne’er trust me then, for when a world of men<br>Could not prevail with all their oratory,<br>Yet hath a woman’s kindness overruled.—<br>And therefore tell her I return great thanks,<br>And in submission will attend on her.—<br>Will not your honours bear me company? |
| BEDFORD | No, truly, ‘tis more than manners will.<br>And I have heard it said, ‘Unbidden guests<br>Are often welcomest when they are gone’. |
| TALBOT | [[Well]] then, atone—since there’s no remedy—<br>I mean to prove this lady’s courtesy.<br>Come hither, captain.<br>He whispers<br>You perceive my mind? |
| CAPTAIN | I do, my lord, and mean accordingly. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Countess of Auvergne and her Porter |
| COUNTESS | Porter, remember what I gave in charge,<br>And when you have done so, bring the keys to me. |
| PORTER | Madam, I will. Exit |
| COUNTESS | The plot is laid. If all things fall out right,<br>I shall as famous be by this exploit<br>As Scythian Tomyris by Cyrus’ [[Death\|death]].<br>Great is the rumour of this dreadful knight,<br>And his achievements of no less account.<br>Fain would mine eyes be witness with mine ears,<br>To give their censure of these rare reports. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Messenger and [[Lord Talbot]] |
| MESSENGER | Madam, according as your ladyship desired,<br>By message craved, so is [[Lord Talbot]] come. |
| COUNTESS | And he is welcome. What, is this the man? |
| MESSENGER | Madam, it is. |
| COUNTESS | Is this the scourge of France?<br>Is this the Talbot, so much feared abroad<br>That with his name the mothers still their babes?<br>I see report is fabulous and false.<br>I thought I should have seen some Hercules,<br>A second [[Hector]], for his grim aspect<br>And large proportion of his strong-knit limbs.<br>Alas, this is a [[Child\|child]], a seely [[Dwarf\|dwarf]].<br>It cannot be this weak and writhled shrimp<br>Should strike such terror to his enemies. |
| TALBOT | Madam, I have been bold to trouble you.<br>But since your ladyship is not at leisure,<br>I’ll sort some other [[Time\|time]] to visit you.<br>He is going |
| COUNTESS | (to Messenger)<br>What means he now? Go ask him whither he goes. |
| MESSENGER | Stay, my [[Lord Talbot]], for my lady craves<br>To know the cause of your abrupt departure. |
| TALBOT | Marry, for that she’s in a wrong belief,<br>I go to certify her Talbot’s here. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Porter with keys |
| COUNTESS | If thou be he, then art thou prisoner. |
| TALBOT | Prisoner? To whom? |
| COUNTESS | To me, bloodthirsty lord;<br>And for that cause I trained thee to my [[house]].<br>Long [[Time\|time]] thy [[Shadow\|shadow]] hath been thrall to me,<br>For in my gallery thy picture hangs;<br>But now the substance shall endure the like,<br>And I will chain these legs and arms of thine<br>That hast by tyranny these many years<br>Wasted our country, slain our citizens,<br>And sent our sons and husbands captivate— |
| TALBOT | Ha, ha, ha! |
| COUNTESS | Laughest thou, wretch? Thy mirth shall turn to moan. |
| TALBOT | I laugh to see your ladyship so fond<br>To think that you have aught but Talbot’s [[Shadow\|shadow]]<br>Whereon to practise your severity. |
| COUNTESS | Why? Art not thou the man? |
| TALBOT | I am indeed. |
| COUNTESS | Then have I substance too. |
| TALBOT | No, no, I am but [[Shadow\|shadow]] of myself.<br>You are deceived; my substance is not here.<br>For what you see is but the smallest part<br>And least proportion of humanity.<br>I tell you, madam, were the whole frame here,<br>It is of such a spacious lofty pitch<br>Your roof were not sufficient to contain’t. |
| COUNTESS | This is a riddling merchant for the nonce.<br>He will be here, and yet he is not here.<br>How can these contrarieties agree? |
| TALBOT | That will I show you presently.<br>He winds his horn. Within, drums strike up; a peal of ordnance. Enter English soldiers<br>How say you, madam? Are you now persuaded<br>That Talbot is but [[Shadow\|shadow]] of himself?<br>These are his substance, sinews, arms, and strength,<br>With which he yoketh your rebellious necks,<br>Razeth your cities and subverts your towns,<br>And in a moment makes them desolate. |
| COUNTESS | Victorious Talbot, pardon my abuse.<br>I find thou art no less than fame hath bruited,<br>And more than may be gathered by thy shape.<br>Let my presumption not provoke thy wrath,<br>For I am sorry that with reverence<br>I did not entertain thee as thou art. |
| TALBOT | Be not dismayed, fair lady, nor misconster<br>The mind of Talbot, as you did mistake<br>The outward composition of his [[Body\|body]].<br>What you have done hath not offended me;<br>Nor other satisfaction do I crave<br>But only, with your patience, that we may<br>Taste of your wine and see what cates you have:<br>For soldiers’ stomachs always serve them [[Well\|well]]. |
| COUNTESS | With all my heart; and think me honoured<br>To feast so great a warrior in my [[house]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | A [[Rose\|rose]] brier. Enter [[Richard]] Plantagenet, the [[Earl of Warwick]], the Duke of Somerset, William de la Pole (the [[Earl of Suffolk]]), Vernon, and a Lawyer |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Great lords and gentlemen, what means this [[Silence\|silence]]?<br>Dare no man answer in a case of [[Truth\|truth]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | Within the Temple hall we were too loud. |
| SUFFOLK | The [[Garden\|garden]] here is more convenient. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Then say at once if I maintained the [[Truth\|truth]];<br>Or else was wrangling Somerset in th’error? |
| SUFFOLK | [[Faith]], I have been a truant in the law,<br>And never yet could frame my will to it,<br>And therefore frame the law unto my will. |
| SOMERSET | Judge you, my lord of Warwick, then between us. |
| WARWICK | Between two hawks, which flies the higher pitch,<br>Between two [[dogs]], which hath the deeper mouth,<br>Between two blades, which bears the better temper,<br>Between two horses, which doth bear him best,<br>Between two girls, which hath the merriest eye,<br>I have perhaps some shallow spirit of judgement;<br>But in these nice sharp quillets of the law,<br>Good [[Faith\|faith]], I am no wiser than a daw. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Tut, tut, here is a mannerly forbearance.<br>The [[Truth\|truth]] appears so naked on my side<br>That any purblind eye may find it out. |
| SOMERSET | And on my side it is so [[Well\|well]] apparelled,<br>So clear, so shining, and so evident,<br>That it will glimmer through a blind man’s eye. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Since you are tongue-tied and so loath to speak,<br>In dumb significants proclaim your thoughts.<br>Let him that is a true-born gentleman<br>And stands upon the [[Honour\|honour]] of his birth,<br>If he suppose that I have pleaded [[Truth\|truth]],<br>From off this briar pluck a white [[Rose\|rose]] with me.<br>He plucks a white [[Rose\|rose]] |
| SOMERSET | Let him that is no coward nor no flatterer,<br>But dare maintain the party of the [[Truth\|truth]],<br>Pluck a red [[Rose\|rose]] from off this thorn with me.<br>He plucks a red [[Rose\|rose]] |
| WARWICK | I [[Love\|love]] no colours, and without all colour<br>Of base insinuating flattery<br>I pluck this white [[Rose\|rose]] with Plantagenet. |
| SUFFOLK | I pluck this red [[Rose\|rose]] with young Somerset,<br>And say withal I think he held the right. |
| VERNON | Stay, lords and gentlemen, and pluck no more<br>Till you conclude that he upon whose side<br>The fewest roses from the tree are cropped<br>Shall yield the other in the right opinion. |
| SOMERSET | Good Master Vernon, it is [[Well\|well]] objected.<br>If I have fewest, I subscribe in [[Silence\|silence]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | And I. |
| VERNON | Then for the [[Truth\|truth]] and plainness of the case<br>I pluck this pale and maiden blossom here,<br>Giving my verdict on the white [[Rose\|rose]]’ side. |
| SOMERSET | Prick not your finger as you pluck it off,<br>Lest, bleeding, you do paint the white [[Rose\|rose]] red,<br>And fall on my side so against your will. |
| VERNON | If I, my lord, for my opinion bleed,<br>Opinion shall be surgeon to my hurt<br>And keep me on the side where still I am. |
| SOMERSET | [[Well]], [[Well\|well]], come on! Who else? |
| LAWYER | Unless my study and my books be false,<br>The argument you held was wrong in law;<br>In sign whereof I pluck a white [[Rose\|rose]] too. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Now Somerset, where is your argument? |
| SOMERSET | Here in my scabbard, meditating that<br>Shall dye your white [[Rose\|rose]] in a bloody red. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Meantime your cheeks do counterfeit our roses,<br>For pale they look with [[Fear\|fear]], as witnessing<br>The [[Truth\|truth]] on our side. |
| SOMERSET | No, Plantagenet,<br>‘Tis not for [[Fear\|fear]], but anger, that thy cheeks<br>Blush for pure shame to counterfeit our roses,<br>And yet thy tongue will not confess thy error. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Hath not thy [[Rose\|rose]] a canker, Somerset? |
| SOMERSET | Hath not thy [[Rose\|rose]] a thorn, Plantagenet? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Ay, sharp and piercing, to maintain his [[Truth\|truth]],<br>Whiles thy consuming canker eats his falsehood. |
| SOMERSET | [[Well]], I’ll find friends to wear my bleeding roses,<br>That shall maintain what I have said is true,<br>Where false Plantagenet dare not be seen. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Now, by this maiden blossom in my hand,<br>I scorn thee and thy fashion, peevish boy. |
| SUFFOLK | Turn not thy scorns this way, Plantagenet. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Proud Pole, I will, and scorn both him and thee. |
| SUFFOLK | I’ll turn my part thereof into thy throat. |
| SOMERSET | Away, away, good William de la Pole.<br>We grace the yeoman by conversing with him. |
| WARWICK | Now, by God’s will, thou wrong’st him, Somerset.<br>His grandfather was Lionel Duke of [[Clarence]],<br>Third son to the third Edward, [[King]] of England.<br>[[Spring]] crestless yeomen from so deep a root? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | He bears him on the place’s privilege,<br>Or durst not for his craven heart say thus. |
| SOMERSET | By him that made me, I’ll maintain my words<br>On any plot of ground in Christendom.<br>Was not thy father, [[Richard]] Earl of Cambridge,<br>For treason executed in our late [[King\|king]]’s days?<br>And by his treason stand’st not thou attainted,<br>Corrupted, and exempt from ancient gentry?<br>His trespass yet lives guilty in thy [[Blood\|blood]],<br>And till thou be restored thou art a yeoman. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | My father was attached, not attainted;<br>Condemned to die for treason, but no traitor—<br>And that I’ll prove on better men than Somerset,<br>Were growing [[Time\|time]] once ripened to my will.<br>For your partaker Pole, and you yourself,<br>I’ll note you in my book of [[Memory\|memory]],<br>To scourge you for this apprehension.<br>Look to it [[Well\|well]], and say you are [[Well\|well]] warned. |
| SOMERSET | Ah, thou shalt find us ready for thee still,<br>And know us by these colours for thy foes,<br>For these my friends, in spite of thee, shall wear. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | And, by my [[Soul\|soul]], this pale and angry [[Rose\|rose]],<br>As cognizance of my [[Blood\|blood]]-drinking hate,<br>Will I forever, and my faction, wear<br>Until it wither with me to my [[Grave\|grave]],<br>Or flourish to the height of my degree. |
| SUFFOLK | Go forward, and be choked with thy [[Ambition\|ambition]].<br>And so farewell until I meet thee next. Exit |
| SOMERSET | Have with thee, Pole.—Farewell, ambitious [[Richard]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | How I am braved, and must perforce endure it! |
| WARWICK | This blot that they object against your [[house]]<br>Shall be wiped out in the next parliament,<br>Called for the truce of Winchester and [[Gloucester]].<br>An if thou be not then created York,<br>I will not live to be accounted Warwick.<br>Meantime, in signal of my [[Love\|love]] to thee.<br>Against proud Somerset and William Pole,<br>Will I upon thy party wear this [[Rose\|rose]].<br>And here I prophesy: this brawl today,<br>Grown to this faction in the Temple [[Garden\|garden]],<br>Shall send, between the red [[Rose\|rose]] and the white,<br>A thousand souls to [[Death\|death]] and deadly [[Night\|night]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Good Master Vernon, I am bound to you,<br>That you on my behalf would pluck a flower. |
| VERNON | In your behalf still will I wear the same. |
| LAWYER | And so will I. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Thanks, gentles.<br>Come, let us four to dinner. I dare say<br>This quarrel will drink [[Blood\|blood]] another day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt. The [[Rose\|rose]] brier is removed |

### Atto 2, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Edmund]] Mortimer, brought in a chair by his Keepers |
| MORTIMER | Kind keepers of my weak decaying age,<br>Let dying Mortimer here rest himself.<br>Even like a man new-haled from the rack,<br>So fare my limbs with long [[Imprisonment\|imprisonment]];<br>And these grey locks, the pursuivants of [[Death\|death]],<br>Argue the end of [[Edmund]] Mortimer,<br>Nestor-like aged in an age of care.<br>These eyes, like lamps whose wasting oil is spent,<br>Wax dim, as drawing to their exigent;<br>Weak shoulders, overborne with burdening grief,<br>And pithless arms, like to a withered vine<br>That droops his sapless branches to the ground.<br>Yet are these feet—whose strengthless stay is numb,<br>Unable to support this lump of clay—<br>Swift-winged with [[Desire\|desire]] to get a [[Grave\|grave]],<br>As witting I no other comfort have.<br>But tell me, keeper, will my nephew come? |
| KEEPER | [[Richard]] Plantagenet, my lord, will come.<br>We sent unto the Temple, unto his chamber,<br>And answer was returned that he will come. |
| MORTIMER | Enough. My [[Soul\|soul]] shall then be satisfied.<br>Poor gentleman, his wrong doth equal mine.<br>Since Henry Monmouth first began to reign—<br>Before whose glory I was great in arms—<br>This loathsome sequestration have I had;<br>And even since then hath [[Richard]] been obscured,<br>Deprived of [[Honour\|honour]] and inheritance.<br>But now the arbitrator of despairs,<br>Just [[Death]], kind umpire of men’s miseries,<br>With sweet enlargement doth dismiss me hence.<br>I would his troubles likewise were expired,<br>That so he might recover what was lost. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Richard]] Plantagenet |
| KEEPER | My lord, your loving nephew now is come. |
| MORTIMER | [[Richard]] Plantagenet, my friend, is he come? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Ay, noble uncle, thus ignobly used:<br>Your nephew, late despised [[Richard]], comes. |
| MORTIMER | (to Keepers)<br>Direct mine arms I may embrace his neck<br>And in his bosom spend my latter gasp.<br>O tell me when my lips do touch his cheeks,<br>That I may kindly give one fainting kiss.<br>He embraces [[Richard]]<br>And now declare, sweet stem from York’s great stock,<br>Why didst thou say of late thou wert despised? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | First lean thine aged back against mine arm,<br>And in that ease I’ll tell thee my dis-ease.<br>This day in argument upon a case<br>Some words there grew ’twixt Somerset and me;<br>Among which terms he used his lavish tongue<br>And did upbraid me with my father’s [[Death\|death]];<br>Which obloquy set bars before my tongue,<br>Else with the like I had requited him.<br>Therefore, good uncle, for my father’s sake,<br>In [[Honour\|honour]] of a true Plantagenet,<br>And for alliance’ sake, declare the cause<br>My father, Earl of Cambridge, lost his head. |
| MORTIMER | That cause, fair nephew, that imprisoned me,<br>And hath detained me all my flow’ring youth |
| *(didascalia)* | Within a loathsome dungeon, there to pine, |
| MORTIMER | Was cursed instrument of his decease. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Discover more at large what cause that was,<br>For I am ignorant and cannot guess. |
| MORTIMER | I will, if that my fading breath permit<br>And [[Death\|death]] approach not ere my tale be done.<br>Henry the Fourth, grandfather to this [[King]],<br>Deposed his nephew [[Richard]], Edward’s son,<br>The first begotten and the lawful heir<br>Of Edward [[King\|king]], the third of that descent;<br>During whose reign the Percies of the north,<br>Finding his usurpation most unjust,<br>Endeavoured my advancement to the throne.<br>The reason moved these warlike lords to this<br>Was for that—[[Young King\|young King]] [[Richard]] thus removed,<br>Leaving no heir begotten of his [[Body\|body]]—<br>I was the next by birth and parentage,<br>For by my mother I derived am<br>From Lionel Duke of [[Clarence]], the third son<br>To [[King]] Edward the Third—whereas the [[King]]<br>From [[John of Gaunt]] doth bring his pedigree,<br>Being but fourth of that heroic line.<br>But mark: as in this haughty great attempt<br>They laboured to plant the rightful heir,<br>I lost my liberty, and they their lives.<br>Long after this, when Henry the Fifth,<br>Succeeding his father Bolingbroke, did reign,<br>Thy father, Earl of Cambridge then, derived<br>From famous [[Edmund]] Langley, [[Duke of York]],<br>Marrying my sister that thy mother was,<br>Again, in pity of my hard distress,<br>Levied an army, weening to redeem<br>And have installed me in the diadem;<br>But, as the rest, so fell that noble earl,<br>And was beheaded. Thus the Mortimers,<br>In whom the title rested, were suppressed. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Of which, my lord, your [[Honour\|honour]] is the last. |
| MORTIMER | True, and thou seest that I no issue have,<br>And that my fainting words do warrant [[Death\|death]].<br>Thou art my heir. The rest I wish thee gather—<br>But yet be wary in thy studious care. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Thy [[Grave\|grave]] admonishments prevail with me.<br>But yet methinks my father’s execution<br>Was nothing less than bloody tyranny. |
| MORTIMER | With [[Silence\|silence]], nephew, be thou politic.<br>Strong-fixed is the [[house]] of Lancaster,<br>And like a mountain, not to be removed.<br>But now thy uncle is removing hence,<br>As princes do their courts, when they are cloyed<br>With long continuance in a settled place. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | O uncle, would some part of my young years<br>Might but redeem the passage of your age. |
| MORTIMER | Thou dost then wrong me, as that slaughterer doth<br>Which giveth many wounds when one will kill.<br>Mourn not, except thou sorrow for my good.<br>Only give order for my funeral.<br>And so farewell, and fair be all thy hopes,<br>And prosperous be thy life in peace and war. Dies |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | And peace, no war, befall thy parting [[Soul\|soul]].<br>In [[Prison\|prison]] hast thou spent a pilgrimage,<br>And like a hermit overpassed thy days.<br>[[Well]], I will lock his counsel in my breast,<br>And what I do imagine, let that rest.<br>Keepers, convey him hence, and I myself<br>Will see his burial better than his life. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Keepers with Mortimer’s [[Body\|body]] |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Here dies the dusky torch of Mortimer,<br>Choked with [[Ambition\|ambition]] of the meaner sort.<br>And for those wrongs, those bitter injuries,<br>Which Somerset hath offered to my [[house]],<br>I [[Doubt\|doubt]] not but with [[Honour\|honour]] to redress.<br>And therefore haste I to the Parliament,<br>Either to be restored to my [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Or make mine ill th’advantage of my good. Exit |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter [[Young King\|young King]] Henry, the Dukes of Exeter and [[Gloucester]], the Bishop of Winchester; the Duke of Somerset and the [[Earl of Suffolk]] with red roses; the [[Earl of Warwick]] and [[Richard]] Plantagenet with white roses. [[Gloucester]] offers to put up a bill; Winchester snatches it, [[Tears\|tears]] it |
| WINCHESTER | Com‘st thou with deep premeditated lines?<br>With written pamphlets studiously devised?<br>Humphrey of [[Gloucester]], if thou canst accuse,<br>Or aught intend’st to lay unto my charge,<br>Do it without invention, suddenly,<br>As I with sudden and extemporal speech<br>Purpose to answer what thou canst object. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Presumptuous priest, this place commands my<br>patience,<br>Or thou shouldst find thou hast dishonoured me.<br>Think not, although in writing I preferred<br>The manner of thy vile outrageous crimes,<br>That therefore I have forged, or am not able<br>Verbatim to rehearse the method of my pen.<br>No, prelate, such is thy audacious wickedness,<br>Thy lewd, pestiferous, and dissentious pranks,<br>As very infants prattle of thy [[Pride\|pride]].<br>Thou art a most pernicious usurer,<br>Froward by [[Nature\|nature]], enemy to peace,<br>Lascivious, wanton, more than [[Well\|well]] beseems<br>A man of thy profession and degree.<br>And for thy treachery, what’s more manifest?—<br>In that thou laid’st a trap to take my life,<br>As [[Well\|well]] at [[London]] Bridge as at the Tower.<br>Beside, I [[Fear\|fear]] me, if thy thoughts were sifted,<br>The [[King]] thy sovereign is not quite exempt<br>From envious malice of thy swelling heart. |
| WINCHESTER | [[Gloucester]], I do defy thee.—Lords, vouchsafe<br>To give me hearing what I shall reply.<br>If I were covetous, ambitious, or perverse,<br>As he will have me, how am I so poor?<br>Or how haps it I seek not to advance<br>Or raise myself, but keep my wonted calling?<br>And for dissension, who preferreth peace<br>More than I do ?—except I be provoked.<br>No, my good lords, it is not that offends;<br>It is not that that hath incensed the Duke.<br>It is because no one should sway but he,<br>No one but he should be about the [[King]]—<br>And that engenders thunder in his breast<br>And makes him roar these accusations forth.<br>But he shall know I am as good— |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | As good?—<br>Thou bastard of my grandfather. |
| WINCHESTER | Ay, lordly sir; for what are you, I pray,<br>But one imperious in another’s throne? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Am I not Protector, saucy priest? |
| WINCHESTER | And am not I a prelate of the Church? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Yes—as an outlaw in a [[Castle\|castle]] keeps<br>And useth it to patronage his theft. |
| WINCHESTER | Unreverent [[Gloucester]]. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Thou art reverend<br>Touching thy spiritual function, not thy life. |
| WINCHESTER | Rome shall remedy this. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] Roam thither then. |
| *(didascalia)* | WARWICK (to Winchester) |
| WINCHESTER | My lord, it were your duty to forbear. |
| SOMERSET | Ay, so the bishop be not overborne:<br>Methinks my lord should be religious,<br>And know the office that belongs to such. |
| WARWICK | Methinks his lordship should be humbler.<br>It fitteth not a prelate so to plead. |
| SOMERSET | Yes, when his holy state is touched so near. |
| WARWICK | State holy or unhallowed, what of that?<br>Is not his grace Protector to the [[King]]? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | (aside)<br>Plantagenet, I see, must hold his tongue,<br>Lest it be said, ‘Speak, sirrah, when you should;<br>Must your bold verdict intertalk with lords?’<br>Else would I have a fling at Winchester. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Uncles of [[Gloucester]] and of Winchester,<br>The special watchmen of our English weal,<br>I would prevail, if prayers might prevail,<br>To join your hearts in [[Love\|love]] and amity.<br>O what a scandal is it to our [[Crown\|crown]]<br>That two such noble peers as ye should jar!<br>Believe me, lords, my tender years can tell<br>Civil dissension is a viperous worm<br>That gnaws the bowels of the commonwealth. |
| *(didascalia)* | A noise within |
| *(didascalia)* | SERVINGMEN (within) Down with the tawny coats! |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | What tumult’s this? |
| WARWICK | An uproar, I dare warrant,<br>Begun through malice of the Bishop’s men. |
| *(didascalia)* | A noise again |
| *(didascalia)* | SERVINGMEN (within) Stones, stones! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Mayor of [[London]] |
| MAYOR | O my good lords, and virtuous Henry,<br>Pity the [[City\|city]] of [[London]], pity us! so<br>The Bishop and the [[Duke of Gloucester]]’s men,<br>Forbidden late to carry any weapon,<br>Have filled their pockets full of pebble stones<br>And, banding themselves in contrary parts,<br>Do pelt so fast at one another’s pate<br>That many have their giddy brains knocked out.<br>Our windows are broke down in every street,<br>And we for [[Fear\|fear]] compelled to shut our shops. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter in skirmish, with bloody pates, Winchester’s Servingmen in tawny coats and [[Gloucester]]’s in blue coats |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | We charge you, on allegiance to ourself,<br>To hold your slaught’ring hands and keep the peace. |
| *(didascalia)* | The skirmish ceases |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Pray, Uncle [[Gloucester]], mitigate this strife. |
| FIRST SERVINGMAN | Nay, if we be forbidden stones, we’ll fall to it with our teeth. |
| SECOND SERVINGMAN | Do what ye dare, we are as resolute.<br>Skirmish again |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | You of my household, leave this peevish broil,<br>And set this unaccustomed fight aside. |
| THIRD SERVINGMAN | My lord, we know your grace to be a man<br>Just and upright and, for your royal birth,<br>Inferior to none but to his majesty;<br>And ere that we will suffer such a prince,<br>So kind a father of the commonweal,<br>To be disgraced by an inkhorn mate,<br>We and our wives and children all will fight<br>And have our bodies slaughtered by thy foes. |
| FIRST SERVINGMAN | Ay, and the very parings of our nails<br>Shall pitch a field when we are dead.<br>They begin to skirmish again |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Stay, stay, I say!<br>An if you [[Love\|love]] me as you say you do,<br>Let me persuade you to forbear a while. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | O how this discord doth afflict my [[Soul\|soul]]!<br>Can you, my lord of Winchester, behold o<br>My sighs and [[Tears\|tears]], and will not once relent?<br>Who should be pitiful if you be not?<br>Or who should study to prefer a peace,<br>If holy churchmen take delight in broils? |
| WARWICK | Yield, my lord Protector; yield, Winchester—<br>Except you mean with obstinate repulse<br>To slay your sovereign and destroy the realm.<br>You see what mischief-and what murder, too—<br>Hath been enacted through your enmity.<br>Then be at peace, except ye thirst for [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| WINCHESTER | He shall submit, or I will never yield. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Compassion on the [[King]] commands me stoop,<br>Or I would see his heart out ere the priest<br>Should ever get that privilege of me. |
| WARWICK | Behold, my lord of Winchester, the Duke<br>Hath banished moody discontented fury,<br>As by his smoothed brows it doth appear.<br>Why look you still so stern and tragical? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Here, Winchester, I offer thee my hand. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (to Winchester)<br>Fie, Uncle Beaufort! I have heard you preach<br>That malice was a great and grievous sin;<br>And will not you maintain the thing you teach,<br>But prove a chief offender in the same? |
| WARWICK | Sweet [[King]]! The Bishop hath a kindly gird.<br>For shame, my lord of Winchester, relent.<br>What, shall a [[Child\|child]] instruct you what to do? |
| WINCHESTER | [[Well]], [[Duke of Gloucester]], I will yield to thee<br>[[Love]] for thy [[Love\|love]], and hand for hand I give. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | (aside)<br>Ay, but I [[Fear\|fear]] me with a hollow heart.<br>(To the others) See here, my friends and loving<br>countrymen,<br>This token serveth for a [[Flag\|flag]] of truce<br>Betwixt ourselves and all our followers.<br>So help me God, as I dissemble not. |
| WINCHESTER | So help me God (aside) as I intend it not. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | O loving uncle, kind [[Duke of Gloucester]],<br>How joyful am I made by this contract!<br>(To Servingmen) Away, my masters, trouble us no<br>more,<br>But join in [[Friendship\|friendship]] as your lords have done.<br>first SERVINGMAN Content. I’ll to the surgeon’s. |
| SECOND SERVINGMAN | And so will I. |
| THIRD SERVINGMAN | And I will see what physic the tavern affords. Exeunt the Mayor and Servingmen |
| WARWICK | Accept this scroll, most gracious sovereign,<br>Which in the right of [[Richard]] Plantagenet<br>We do exhibit to your majesty. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | [[Well]] urged, my lord of Warwick—for, sweet prince,<br>An if your grace mark every circumstance,<br>You have great reason to do [[Richard]] right,<br>Especially for those occasions<br>At Eltham Place I told your majesty. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And those occasions, uncle, were of force.—<br>Therefore, my loving lords, our pleasure is<br>That [[Richard]] be restored to his [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| WARWICK | Let [[Richard]] be restored to his [[Blood\|blood]].<br>So shall his father’s wrongs be recompensed. |
| WINCHESTER | As will the rest, so willeth Winchester. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | If [[Richard]] will be true, not that [[Alone\|alone]]<br>But all the whole inheritance I give<br>That doth belong unto the [[house]] of York,<br>From whence you [[Spring\|spring]] by lineal descent. 170 |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] PLANTAGENET | Thy humble servant vows obedience<br>And humble service till the point of [[Death\|death]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Stoop then, and set your knee against my foot.<br>[[Richard]] kneels<br>And in reguerdon of that duty done,<br>I gird thee with the valiant [[Sword\|sword]] of York.<br>Rise, [[Richard]], like a true Plantagenet,<br>And rise created princely [[Duke of York]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | (rising)<br>And so thrive [[Richard]], as thy foes may fall;<br>And as my duty springs, so perish they<br>That grudge one thought against your majesty. |
| ALL BUT [[Richard\|RICHARD]] AND | SOMERSET<br>Welcome, high prince, the mighty [[Duke of York]]! |
| SOMERSET | (aside)<br>Perish, base prince, ignoble [[Duke of York]]! |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Now will it best avail your majesty<br>To [[Cross\|cross]] the seas and to be crowned in France.<br>The presence of a [[King\|king]] engenders [[Love\|love]]<br>Amongst his subjects and his loyal friends,<br>As it disanimates his enemies. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | When [[Gloucester]] says the word, [[King]] Henry goes,<br>For friendly counsel cuts off many foes. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Your ships already are in readiness. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sennet. Exeunt all but Exeter |
| EXETER | Ay, we may march in England or in France,<br>Not seeing what is likely to ensue.<br>This late dissension grown betwixt the peers<br>Burns under feigned ashes of forged [[Love\|love]],<br>And will at last break out into a flame.<br>As festered members rot but by degree<br>Till bones and flesh and sinews fall away,<br>So will this base and envious discord breed.<br>And now I [[Fear\|fear]] that fatal prophecy<br>Which, in the [[Time\|time]] of Henry named the Fifth,<br>Was in the mouth of every sucking babe:<br>That ‘Henry born at Monmouth should win all,<br>And Henry born at Windsor should lose all’—<br>Which is so plain that Exeter doth wish<br>His days may finish, ere that hapless [[Time\|time]]. Exit |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Joan la Pucelle]], disguised, with four French Soldiers with sacks upon their backs |
