| JOAN | These are the [[City\|city]] gates, the gates of Rouen,<br>Through which our policy must make a breach.<br>Take heed. Be wary how you place your words.<br>Talk like the vulgar sort of market men<br>That come to gather money for their corn.<br>If we have entrance, as I [[Hope\|hope]] we shall,<br>And that we find the slothful watch but weak,<br>I’ll by a sign give notice to our friends,<br>That [[Charles the Dauphin]] may encounter them.<br>A SOLDIER<br>Our sacks shall be a mean to sack the [[City\|city]],<br>And we be lords and rulers over Rouen.<br>Therefore we’ll knock.<br>They knock |
| WATCH | (within)<br>Qui là? |
| JOAN | Paysans, la pauvre gens de France:<br>Poor market folks that come to sell their corn. |
| WATCH | (opening the gates) |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter, go in. The market bell is rung. |
| JOAN | (aside)<br>Now, Rouen, I’ll shake thy bulwarks to the ground. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]], the Bastard of Orléans, the Duke of Alençon, René Duke of Anjou, and French soldier |
| CHARLES | Saint Denis bless this happy stratagem,<br>And once again we’ll [[Sleep\|sleep]] secure in Rouen. |
| BASTARD | Here entered Pucelle and her practisants.<br>Now she is there, how will she specify<br>‘Here is the best and safest passage in’? |
| RENE | By thrusting out a torch from yonder tower—<br>Which, once discerned, shows that her meaning is:<br>No way to that, for weakness, which she entered. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Joan la Pucelle]] on the top, thrusting out a torch burning |
| JOAN | Behold, this is the happy wedding torch<br>That joineth Rouen unto her countrymen,<br>But burning fatal to the Talbonites. |
| BASTARD | See, noble Charles, the beacon of our friend.<br>The burning torch in yonder turret stands. |
| CHARLES | Now shine it like a comet of [[Revenge\|revenge]],<br>A [[Prophet\|prophet]] to the fall of all our foes! |
| RENE | Defer no [[Time\|time]]; delays have dangerous ends. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter and cry, ‘The [[Dauphin]]!’, presently, |
| RENE | And then do execution on the watch. Alarum. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | An alarum. Enter [[Lord Talbot]] in an excursion |
| TALBOT | France, thou shalt rue this treason with thy [[Tears\|tears]],<br>If Talbot but survive thy treachery.<br>Pucelle, that [[Witch\|witch]], that damnèd sorceress,<br>Hath wrought this hellish mischief unawares,<br>That hardly we escaped the [[Pride\|pride]] of France. Exit |

### Atto 3, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | An alarum. Excursions. The Duke of Bedford brought in sick, in a chair. Enter [[Lord Talbot]] and the Duke of Burgundy, without, within, [[Joan la Pucelle]], [[Charles the Dauphin]], the Bastard of Orléans, the Duke of Alençon, and René Duke of Anjou on the walls |
| JOAN | Good morrow gallants. Want ye corn for bread?<br>I think the Duke of Burgundy will fast<br>Before he’ll buy again at such a rate.<br>‘Twas full of darnel. Do you like the taste? |
| BURGUNDY | Scoff on, vile fiend and shameless courtesan.<br>I trust ere long to choke thee with thine own,<br>And make thee curse the harvest of that corn. |
| CHARLES | Your grace may starve, perhaps, before that [[Time\|time]]. |
| BEDFORD | O let no words, but deeds, [[Revenge\|revenge]] this treason. |
| JOAN | What will you do, good graybeard? Break a lance<br>And run a-tilt at [[Death\|death]] within a chair? |
| TALBOT | Foul fiend of France, and hag of all despite,<br>Encompassed with thy lustful paramours,<br>Becomes it thee to taunt his valiant age<br>And twit with cowardice a man half dead?<br>[[Damsel]], I’ll have a bout with you again,<br>Or else let Talbot perish with this shame. |
| JOAN | Are ye so hot, sir?—Yet, Pucelle, hold thy peace.<br>If Talbot do but thunder, rain will follow.<br>The English whisper together in counsel<br>God [[Speed\|speed]] the parliament; who shall be the Speaker? |
| TALBOT | Dare ye come forth and meet us in the field? |
| JOAN | Belike your lordship takes us then for fools,<br>To try if that our own be ours or no. |
| TALBOT | I speak not to that railing Hecate<br>But unto thee, Alençon, and the rest.<br>Will ye, like soldiers, come and fight it out?<br>ALENÇON<br>Seignieur, no. |
| TALBOT | Seignieur, hang! Base muleteers of France, Like peasant footboys do they keep the walls And dare not take up arms like gentlemen. |
| JOAN | Away, captains, let’s get us from the walls,<br>For Talbot means no goodness by his looks.<br>Goodbye, my lord. We came but to tell you<br>That we are here. Exeunt French from the walls |
| TALBOT | And there will we be, too, ere it be long,<br>Or else reproach be Talbot’s greatest fame.<br>Vow Burgundy, by [[Honour\|honour]] of thy [[house]],<br>Pricked on by public wrongs sustained in France,<br>Either to get the town again or die.<br>And I—as sure as English Henry lives,<br>And as his father here was conqueror;<br>As sure as in this late betrayed town<br>Great Cceur-de-[[Lion\|lion]]’s heart was burièd—<br>So sure I swear to get the town or die. |
| BURGUNDY | My vows are equal partners with thy vows. |
| TALBOT | But ere we go, regard this dying prince,<br>The valiant Duke of Bedford. (To Bedford) Come, my<br>lord,<br>We will bestow you in some better place,<br>Fitter for sickness and for crazy age. |
| BEDFORD | [[Lord Talbot]], do not so dishonour me.<br>Here will I sit before the walls of Rouen,<br>And will be partner of your weal or woe. |
| BURGUNDY | Courageous Bedford, let us now persuade you. |
| BEDFORD | Not to be gone from hence; for once I read<br>That stout Pendragon, in his litter sick,<br>Came to the field and vanquishèd his foes.<br>Methinks I should revive the soldiers’ hearts,<br>Because I ever found them as myself. |
| TALBOT | Undaunted spirit in a dying breast!<br>Then be it so; heavens keep old Bedford safe.<br>And now no more ado, brave Burgundy,<br>But gather we our forces out of hand,<br>And set upon our boasting enemy. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with Burgundy |
| TALBOT | An alarum. Excursions. Enter Sir John Fastolf and a<br>Captain |
| CAPTAIN | Whither away, Sir John Fastolf, in such haste? |
| FASTOLF | Whither away? To save myself by flight.<br>We are like to have the overthrow again. |
| CAPTAIN | What, will you fly, and leave [[Lord Talbot]]? |
| FASTOLF | Ay, all the Talbots in the world, to save my life. Exit |
| CAPTAIN | Cowardly knight, ill fortune follow thee! Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Retreat. Excursions. Joan, Alençon, and Charles fly |
| BEDFORD | Now, quiet [[Soul\|soul]], depart when heaven please,<br>For I have seen our enemies’ overthrow.<br>What is the trust or strength of foolish man?<br>They that of late were daring with their scoffs<br>Are glad and fain by flight to save themselves.<br>Bedford dies, and is carried in by two in his chair |

### Atto 3, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | An alarum. Enter [[Lord Talbot]], the Duke of Burgundy, and the rest of the English soldiers |
| TALBOT | Lost and recovered in a day again!<br>This is a [[Double\|double]] [[Honour\|honour]], Burgundy;<br>Yet heavens have glory for this victory! |
| BURGUNDY | Warlike and martial Talbot, Burgundy<br>Enshrines thee in his heart, and there erects<br>Thy noble deeds as valour’s monuments. |
| TALBOT | Thanks, gentle Duke. But where is Pucelle now?<br>I think her old familiar is asleep.<br>Now where’s the Bastard’s braves, and Charles his<br>gleeks?<br>What, all amort? Rouen hangs her head for grief<br>That such a valiant company are fled.<br>Now will we take some order in the town,<br>Placing therein some expert officers,<br>And then depart to [[Paris]], to the [[King]],<br>For there young Henry with his nobles lie. |
| BURGUNDY | What wills [[Lord Talbot]] pleaseth Burgundy. |
| TALBOT | But yet, before we go, let’s not forget<br>The noble Duke of Bedford late deceased,<br>But see his exequies fulfilled in Rouen.<br>A braver soldier never couched lance;<br>A gentler heart did never sway in [[Court\|court]].<br>But kings and mightiest potentates must die,<br>For that’s the end of human misery. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]], the Bastard of Orléans, the Duke of Alençon, [[Joan la Pucelle]], and French soldiers |
| JOAN | Dismay not, princes, at this accident,<br>Nor grieve that Rouen is so recovered.<br>Care is no cure, but rather corrosive,<br>For things that are not to be remedied.<br>Let frantic Talbot triumph for a while,<br>And like a peacock sweep along his tail;<br>We’ll pull his plumes and take away his train,<br>If [[Dauphin]] and the rest will be but ruled. |
| CHARLES | We have been guided by thee hitherto,<br>And of thy cunning had no diffidence.<br>One sudden foil shall never breed distrust. |
| BASTARD | (to Joan)<br>Search out thy wit for secret policies,<br>And we will make thee famous through the world.<br>ALENÇON (to Joan)<br>We’ll set thy statue in some holy place<br>And have thee reverenced like a blessed saint.<br>Employ thee then, sweet virgin, for our good. |
| JOAN | Then thus it must be; this doth Joan devise:<br>By fair persuasions mixed with sugared words<br>We will entice the Duke of Burgundy<br>To leave the Talbot and to follow us. 20 |
| CHARLES | Ay, marry, sweeting, if we could do that<br>France were no place for Henry’s warriors,<br>Nor should that nation boast it so with us,<br>But be extirpèd from our provinces.<br>ALENÇON<br>For ever should they be expulsed from France<br>And not have title of an earldom here. |
| JOAN | Your honours shall perceive how I will work<br>To bring this matter to the wished end. |
| *(didascalia)* | Drum sounds afar off |
| JOAN | Hark, by the sound of drum you may perceive<br>Their powers are marching unto [[Paris]]-ward.<br>Here sound an English march<br>There goes the Talbot, with his colours spread,<br>And all the troops of English after him.<br>Here sound a French march<br>Now in the rearward comes the Duke and his;<br>Fortune in favour makes him lag behind.<br>Summon a parley. We will talk with him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Trumpets sound a |
| JOAN | parley |
| *(didascalia)* | CHARLES calling |
| JOAN | A parley with the Duke of Burgundy. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Duke of Burgundy |
| BURGUNDY | Who craves a parley with the Burgundy? |
| JOAN | The princely Charles of France, thy countryman. |
| BURGUNDY | What sayst thou, Charles?—for I am marching hence. |
| CHARLES | Speak, Pucelle, and enchant him with thy words. |
| JOAN | Brave Burgundy, undoubted [[Hope\|hope]] of France,<br>Stay. Let thy humble handmaid speak to thee. |
| BURGUNDY | Speak on, but be not over-tedious. |
| JOAN | Look on thy country, look on fertile France,<br>And see the cities and the towns defaced<br>By wasting ruin of the cruel foe.<br>As looks the mother on her lowly babe<br>When [[Death\|death]] doth close his tender-dying eyes,<br>See, see the pining malady of France;<br>Behold the wounds, the most unnatural wounds,<br>Which thou thyself hast given her woeful breast.<br>O turn thy edged [[Sword\|sword]] another way,<br>Strike those that hurt, and hurt not those that help.<br>One drop of [[Blood\|blood]] drawn from thy country’s bosom<br>Should grieve thee more than streams of foreign gore.<br>Return thee, therefore, with a flood of [[Tears\|tears]],<br>And wash away thy country’s stained spots. |
| *(didascalia)* | BURGUNDY aside |
| JOAN | Either she hath bewitched me with her words,<br>Or [[Nature\|nature]] makes me suddenly relent. |
| JOAN | Besides, all French and France exclaims on thee,<br>Doubting thy birth and lawful progeny.<br>Who join‘st thou with but with a lordly nation<br>That will not trust thee but for profit’s sake?<br>When Talbot hath set footing once in France<br>And fashioned thee that instrument of ill,<br>Who then but English Henry will be lord,<br>And thou be thrust out like a fugitive?<br>Call we to mind, and mark but this for proof:<br>Was not the Duke of Orléans thy foe?<br>And was he not in England prisoner?<br>But when they heard he was thine enemy<br>They set him free, without his ransom paid,<br>In spite of Burgundy and all his friends.<br>See, then, thou fight’st against thy countrymen,<br>And join’st with them will be thy slaughtermen.<br>Come, come, return; return, thou wandering lord,<br>Charles and the rest will take thee in their arms. |
| *(didascalia)* | BURGUNDY aside |
| JOAN | I am vanquished. These haughty words of hers<br>Have battered me like roaring cannon-shot<br>And made me almost yield upon my knees.<br>(To the others) Forgive me, country, and sweet<br>countrymen;<br>And lords, accept this hearty kind embrace.<br>My forces and my power of men are yours.<br>So farewell, Talbot. I’ll no longer trust thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Done like a Frenchman—aside turn and turn again. |
| CHARLES | Welcome, brave Duke. Thy [[Friendship\|friendship]] makes us fresh. |
| BASTARD | And doth beget new [[Courage\|courage]] in our breasts.<br>ALENÇON<br>Pucelle hath bravely played her part in this,<br>And doth deserve a coronet of gold. |
| CHARLES | Now let us on, my lords, and join our powers,<br>And seek how we may prejudice the foe. Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 3.8 Flourish. Enter [[King]] Henry, the [[Duke of Gloucester]], the Bishop of Winchester, the [[Duke of Exeter]]; [[Richard]] [[Duke of York]], the [[Earl of Warwick]], and Vernon with white roses; the [[Earl of Suffolk]], the Duke of Somerset, and Basset with red roses. To them, with his soldiers, enter [[Lord Talbot]] |
| TALBOT | My gracious prince and honourable peers,<br>Hearing of your arrival in this realm<br>I have a while given truce unto my wars<br>To do my duty to my sovereign;<br>In sign whereof, this arm that hath reclaimed<br>To your obedience fifty fortresses,<br>Twelve cities, and seven walled towns of strength,<br>Beside five hundred prisoners of esteem,<br>Lets fall his [[Sword\|sword]] before your highness’ feet,<br>And with submissive loyalty of heart<br>Ascribes the glory of his conquest got<br>First to my God, and next unto your grace. |
| *(didascalia)* | He kneels |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Is this the [[Lord Talbot]], uncle [[Gloucester]],<br>That hath so long been resident in France? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Yes, if it please your majesty, my liege. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (to Talbot)<br>Welcome, brave captain and victorious lord.<br>When I was young—as yet I am not old—<br>I do remember how my father said<br>A stouter champion never handled [[Sword\|sword]].<br>Long since we were resolvèd of your [[Truth\|truth]],<br>Your faithful service and your toil in war,<br>Yet never have you tasted our reward,<br>Or been reguerdoned with so much as thanks,<br>Because till now we never saw your face.<br>Therefore stand up,<br>Talbot rises<br>and for these good deserts<br>We here create you Earl of Shrewsbury;<br>And in our coronation take your place. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sennet. Exeunt all but Vernon and Basset |
| VERNON | Now sir, to you that were so hot at sea,<br>Disgracing of these colours that I wear<br>In [[Honour\|honour]] of my noble lord of York,<br>Dar’st thou maintain the former words thou spak’st? |
| BASSET | Yes, sir, as [[Well\|well]] as you dare patronage<br>The envious barking of your saucy tongue<br>Against my lord the Duke of Somerset. |
| VERNON | Sirrah, thy lord I [[Honour\|honour]] as he is. |
| BASSET | Why, what is he?—as good a man as York. |
| VERNON | Hark ye, not so. In witness, take ye that.<br>Vernon strikes him |
| BASSET | [[Villain]], thou know‘st the law of arms is such<br>That whoso draws a [[Sword\|sword]] ’tis present [[Death\|death]],<br>Or else this blow should broach thy dearest [[Blood\|blood]].<br>But I’ll unto his majesty and crave<br>I may have liberty to venge this wrong,<br>When thou shalt see I’ll meet thee to thy cost. |
| VERNON | [[Well]], miscreant, I’ll be there as soon as you,<br>And after meet you sooner than you would. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 4.1 Flourish. Enter [[King]] Henry, the [[Duke of Gloucester]], the Bishop of Winchester, the [[Duke of Exeter]],; [[Richard]] [[Duke of York]], and the [[Earl of Warwick]] with white roses; the [[Earl of Suffolk]] and the Duke of Somerset with red roses; [[Lord Talbot]], and the Governor of [[Paris]] |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Lord Bishop, set the [[Crown\|crown]] upon his head. |
| WINCHESTER | God save [[King]] Henry, of that name the sixth I<br>Winchester crowns the [[King]] |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Now, Governor of [[Paris]], take your oath<br>That you elect no other [[King\|king]] but him;<br>Esteem none friends but such as are his friends,<br>And none your foes but such as shall pretend<br>Malicious practices against his state.<br>This shall ye do, so help you righteous God. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John Fastolf with a letter |
| FASTOLF | My gracious sovereign, as I rode from Calais<br>To haste unto your coronation<br>A letter was delivered to my hands, |
| *(didascalia)* | He presents the letters |
| FASTOLF | Writ to your grace from th’ Duke of Burgundy. |
| TALBOT | Shame to the Duke of Burgundy and thee!<br>I vowed, base knight, when I did meet thee next,<br>To tear the Garter from thy craven’s leg,<br>He [[Tears\|tears]] it off<br>Which I have done because unworthily<br>Thou wast installèd in that high degree.—<br>Pardon me, princely Henry and the rest.<br>This dastard at the battle of Patay<br>When but in all I was six thousand strong,<br>And that the French were almost ten to one,<br>Before we met, or that a stroke was given,<br>Like to a trusty squire did run away;<br>In which assault we lost twelve hundred men.<br>Myself and divers gentlemen beside<br>Were there surprised and taken prisoners.<br>Then judge, great lords, if I have done amiss,<br>Or whether that such cowards ought to wear<br>This ornament of knighthood: yea or no? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | To say the [[Truth\|truth]], this fact was infamous<br>And ill beseeming any [[Common Man\|common man]],<br>Much more a knight, a captain and a leader. |
| TALBOT | When first this order was ordained, my lords,<br>Knights of the Garter were of noble birth,<br>Valiant and virtuous, full of haughty [[Courage\|courage]],<br>Such as were grown to credit by the wars;<br>Not fearing [[Death\|death]] nor shrinking for distress,<br>But always resolute in most extremes.<br>He then that is not furnished in this sort<br>Doth but usurp the sacred name of knight,<br>Profaning this most honourable order,<br>And should—if I were worthy to be judge—<br>Be quite degraded, like a hedge-born swain<br>That doth presume to boast of gentle [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (to Fastolf)<br>Stain to thy countrymen, thou hear’st thy doom.<br>Be packing, therefore, thou that wast a knight.<br>Henceforth we banish thee on [[Pain\|pain]] of [[Death\|death]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Fastolf |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And now, my Lord Protector, view the letter<br>Sent from our uncle, Duke of Burgundy. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | What means his grace that he hath changed his<br>style?<br>No more but plain and bluntly ‘To the [[King]]’?<br>Hath he forgot he is his sovereign?<br>Or doth this churlish superscription<br>Pretend some alteration in good will?<br>What’s here? ‘I have upon especial cause,<br>Moved with compassion of my country’s wrack<br>Together with the pitiful complaints<br>Of such as your oppression feeds upon,<br>Forsaken your pernicious faction<br>And joined with Charles, the rightful [[King of France]].’<br>O monstrous treachery! Can this be so?<br>That in alliance, amity, and oaths<br>There should be found such false dissembling guile? |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | What? Doth my uncle Burgundy revolt? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | He doth, my lord, and is become your foe. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Is that the worst this letter doth contain? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | It is the worst, and all, my lord, he writes. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Why then, [[Lord Talbot]] there shall talk with him<br>And give him chastisement for this abuse.<br>(To Talbot) How say you, my lord? Are you not<br>content? |
| TALBOT | Content, my liege? Yes. But that I am prevented,<br>I should have begged I might have been employed. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Then gather strength and march unto him straight.<br>Let him perceive how ill we brook his treason,<br>And what offence it is to flout his friends. |
| TALBOT | I go, my lord, in heart desiring still<br>You may behold confusion of your foes. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Vernon wearing a white [[Rose\|rose]], and Basset wearing a red [[Rose\|rose]] |
| VERNON | (to [[King]] Henry)<br>Grant me the combat, gracious sovereign. |
| BASSET | (to [[King]] Henry)<br>And me, my lord; grant me the combat, too. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | (to [[King]] Henry, pointing to Vernon)<br>This is my servant; hear him, noble Prince. |
| SOMERSET | (to [[King]] Henry, pointing to Basset)<br>And this is mine, sweet Henry; favour him. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Be patient, lords, and give them leave to speak.<br>Say, gentlemen, what makes you thus exclaim,<br>And wherefore crave you combat, or with whom? |
| VERNON | With him, my lord; for he hath done me wrong. |
| BASSET | And I with him; for he hath done me wrong. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | What is that wrong whereof you both complain?<br>First let me know, and then I’ll answer you. |
| BASSET | Crossing the sea from England into France,<br>This fellow here with envious carping tongue<br>Upbraided me about the [[Rose\|rose]] I wear,<br>Saying the sanguine colour of the [[Leaves\|leaves]]<br>Did represent my master’s blushing cheeks<br>When stubbornly he did repugn the [[Truth\|truth]]<br>About a certain question in the law<br>Argued betwixt the [[Duke of York]] and him,<br>With other vile and ignominious terms;<br>In confutation of which rude reproach,<br>And in defence of my lord’s worthiness,<br>I crave the benefit of law of arms. |
| VERNON | And that is my petition, noble lord;<br>For though he seem with forged quaint conceit<br>To set a gloss upon his bold intent,<br>Yet know, my lord, I was provoked by him,<br>And he first took exceptions at this badge,<br>Pronouncing that the paleness of this flower<br>Bewrayed the faintness of my master’s heart. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Will not this malice, Somerset, be left? |
| SOMERSET | Your private grudge, my lord of York, will out,<br>Though ne’er so cunningly you smother it. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Good Lord, what [[Madness\|madness]] rules in brainsick men<br>When for so slight and frivolous a cause<br>Such factious emulations shall arise?<br>Good cousins both of York and Somerset,<br>Quiet yourselves, I pray, and be at peace. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Let this dissension first be tried by fight,<br>And then your highness shall command a peace. |
| SOMERSET | The quarrel toucheth none but us [[Alone\|alone]];<br>Betwixt ourselves let us decide it then. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | There is my pledge. Accept it, Somerset. |
| VERNON | (to [[King]] Henry)<br>Nay, let it rest where it began at first. |
| BASSET | (to [[King]] Henry)<br>Confirm it so, mine honourable lord. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Confirm it so? Confounded be your strife,<br>And perish ye with your audacious prate!<br>Presumptuous vassals, are you not ashamed<br>With this immodest clamorous outrage<br>To trouble and disturb the [[King]] and us?<br>And you, my lords, methinks you do not [[Well\|well]]<br>To bear with their perverse objections,<br>Much less to take occasion from their mouths<br>To raise a mutiny betwixt yourselves.<br>Let me persuade you take a better course. |
| EXETER | It grieves his highness. Good my lords, be friends. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Come hither, you that would be combatants.<br>Henceforth I charge you, as you [[Love\|love]] our favour,<br>Quite to forget this quarrel and the cause.<br>And you, my lords, remember where we are—<br>In France, amongst a fickle wavering nation.<br>If they perceive dissension in our looks,<br>And that within ourselves we disagree,<br>How will their grudging stomachs be provoked<br>To wilful disobedience, and [[Rebel\|rebel]]!<br>Beside, what infamy will there arise<br>When foreign princes shall be certified<br>That for a toy, a thing of no regard,<br>[[King]] Henry’s peers and chief nobility<br>Destroyed themselves and lost the realm of France!<br>O, think upon the conquest of my father,<br>My tender years, and let us not forgo<br>That for a trifle that was bought with [[Blood\|blood]].<br>Let me be umpire in this doubtful strife.<br>I see no reason, if I wear this [[Rose\|rose]],<br>He takes a red [[Rose\|rose]]<br>That anyone should therefore be suspicious<br>I more incline to Somerset than York.<br>Both are my kinsmen, and I [[Love\|love]] them both.<br>As [[Well\|well]] they may upbraid me with my [[Crown\|crown]]<br>Because, forsooth, the [[King]] of Scots is crowned.<br>But your discretions better can persuade<br>Than I am able to instruct or teach,<br>And therefore, as we hither came in peace,<br>So let us still continue peace and [[Love\|love]].<br>Cousin of York, we institute your grace<br>To be our regent in these parts of France;<br>And good my lord of Somerset, unite<br>Your troops of horsemen with his bands of foot,<br>And like true subjects, sons of your progenitors,<br>Go cheerfully together and digest<br>Your angry choler on your enemies.<br>Ourself, my Lord Protector, and the rest,<br>After some respite, will return to Calais,<br>From thence to England, where I [[Hope\|hope]] ere long<br>To be presented by your victories<br>With Charles, Alençon, and that traitorous rout. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Exeunt all but York, Warwick, |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Vernon, and Exeter |
| WARWICK | My lord of York, I promise you, the [[King]]<br>Prettily, methought, did play the orator. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | And so he did; but yet I like it not<br>In that he wears the badge of Somerset. |
| WARWICK | Tush, that was but his fancy; blame him not.<br>I dare presume, sweet Prince, he thought no harm. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | An if I wist he did—but let it rest.<br>Other affairs must now be managed. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but Exeter |
| EXETER | [[Well]] didst thou, [[Richard]], to suppress thy voice;<br>For had the passions of thy heart burst out<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] we should have seen deciphered there<br>More rancorous spite, more furious raging broils,<br>Than yet can be imagined or supposed.<br>But howsoe‘er, no simple man that sees<br>This jarring discord of nobility,<br>This shouldering of each other in the [[Court\|court]],<br>This factious bandying of their favourites,<br>But that it doth presage some ill event.<br>‘Tis much when sceptres are in children’s hands,<br>But more when envy breeds unkind division:<br>There comes the ruin, there begins confusion. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Lord Talbot]] with a trumpeter and drummer and soldiers before Bordeaux |
| TALBOT | Go to the gates of Bordeaux, trumpeter.<br>Summon their general unto the wall.<br>The trumpeter sounds a parley. Enter French General, aloft<br>English John Talbot, captain, calls you forth,<br>Servant in arms to Harry [[King]] of England;<br>And thus he would: open your [[City\|city]] gates,<br>Be humble to us, call my sovereign yours<br>And do him homage as obedient subjects,<br>And I’ll withdraw me and my bloody power.<br>But if you frown upon this proffered peace,<br>You tempt the fury of my three attendants—<br>Lean famine, quartering steel, and climbing [[Fire\|fire]]—<br>Who in a moment even with the earth<br>Shall lay your stately and air-braving towers<br>If you forsake the offer of their [[Love\|love]]. |
| GENERAL | Thou ominous and fearful owl of [[Death\|death]],<br>Our nation’s terror and their bloody scourge,<br>The period of thy tyranny approacheth.<br>On us thou canst not enter but by [[Death\|death]],<br>For I protest we are [[Well\|well]] fortified<br>And strong enough to issue out and fight.<br>If thou retire, the [[Dauphin]] [[Well\|well]] appointed<br>Stands with the snares of war to tangle thee.<br>On either hand thee there are squadrons pitched<br>To wall thee from the liberty of flight,<br>And no way canst thou turn thee for redress<br>But [[Death\|death]] doth front thee with apparent spoil,<br>And pale destruction meets thee in the face.<br>Ten thousand French have ta‘en the sacrament<br>To [[Fire\|fire]] their dangerous artillery<br>Upon no Christian [[Soul\|soul]] but English Talbot.<br>Lo, there thou stand’st, a breathing valiant man<br>Of an invincible unconquered spirit.<br>This is the latest glory of thy praise,<br>That I thy enemy due thee withal,<br>For ere the glass that now begins to run<br>Finish the process of his sandy hour,<br>These eyes that see thee now [[Well\|well]] coloured<br>Shall see thee withered, bloody, pale, and dead. |
| *(didascalia)* | Drum afar off |
| GENERAL | Hark, hark, the [[Dauphin]]’s drum, a warning bell,<br>Sings heavy music to thy timorous [[Soul\|soul]],<br>And mine shall ring thy dire departure out. Exit |
| TALBOT | He fables not. I hear the enemy.<br>Out, some light horsemen, and peruse their wings. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one or more |
| TALBOT | O negligent and heedless discipline,<br>How are we parked and bounded in a pale!—<br>A little herd of England’s timorous deer<br>Mazed with a yelping kennel of French curs.<br>If we be English deer, be then in [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Not rascal-like to fall down with a pinch,<br>But rather, moody-mad and desperate stags,<br>Turn on the bloody hounds with heads of steel<br>And make the cowards stand aloof at bay.<br>Sell every man his life as dear as mine<br>And they shall find dear deer of us, my friends.<br>God and Saint George, Talbot and England’s right,<br>Prosper our colours in this dangerous fight! Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Messenger that meets the [[Duke of York]]. Enter [[Richard]] [[Duke of York]] with a trumpeter and many soldiers |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Are not the speedy scouts returned again<br>That dogged the mighty army of the [[Dauphin]]? |
| MESSENGER | They are returned, my lord, and give it out<br>That he is marched to Bordeaux with his power<br>To fight with Talbot. As he marched along,<br>By your espials were discovered<br>Two mightier troops than that the [[Dauphin]] led,<br>Which joined with him and made their march for<br>Bordeaux. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | A plague upon that [[Villain\|villain]] Somerset<br>That thus delays my promised supply<br>Of horsemen that were levied for this siege!<br>Renowned Talbot doth expect my aid,<br>And I am louted by a traitor [[Villain\|villain]]<br>And cannot help the noble chevalier.<br>God comfort him in this necessity;<br>If he miscarry, farewell wars in France! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter another messenger, Sir William Lucy |
| LUCY | Thou princely leader of our English strength,<br>Never so needful on the earth of France,<br>Spur to the rescue of the noble Talbot,<br>Who now is girdled with a waste of iron<br>And hemmed about with grim destruction.<br>To Bordeaux, warlike Duke; to Bordeaux, York,<br>Else farewell Talbot, France, and England’s [[Honour\|honour]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | O God, that Somerset, who in proud heart<br>Doth stop my cornets, were in Talbot’s place!<br>So should we save a valiant gentleman<br>By forfeiting a traitor and a coward.<br>Mad ire and wrathful fury makes me weep,<br>That thus we die while remiss traitors [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| LUCY | O, send some succour to the distressed lord. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | He dies, we lose; I break my warlike word;<br>We mourn, France smiles; we lose, they daily get,<br>All ‘long of this vile traitor Somerset. |
| LUCY | Then God take [[Mercy\|mercy]] on brave Talbot’s [[Soul\|soul]],<br>And on his son young John, who two hours since<br>I met in travel toward his warlike father.<br>This seven years did not Talbot see his son,<br>And now they meet where both their lives are done. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Alas, what joy shall noble Talbot have<br>To bid his young son welcome to his [[Grave\|grave]]?<br>Away—vexation almost stops my breath<br>That sundered friends greet in the hour of [[Death\|death]].<br>Lucy, farewell. No more my fortune can<br>But curse the cause I cannot aid the man.<br>Maine, Blois, Poitiers, and Tours are won away<br>’Long all of Somerset and his delay. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but Lucy |
| LUCY | Thus while the vulture of sedition<br>Feeds in the bosom of such great commanders,<br>Sleeping neglection doth betray to loss<br>The conquest of our scarce-cold conqueror,<br>That ever-living man of [[Memory\|memory]]<br>Henry the Fifth. Whiles they each other [[Cross\|cross]], |
| *(didascalia)* | Lives, honours, lands, and all hurry to loss. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Duke of Somerset with his army |
| SOMERSET | (to a Captain)<br>It is too late, I cannot send them now.<br>This expedition was by York and Talbot<br>Too rashly plotted. All our general force<br>Might with a sally of the very town<br>Be buckled with. The over-daring Talbot<br>Hath sullied all his gloss of former [[Honour\|honour]]<br>By this unheedful, desperate, wild adventure.<br>York set him on to fight and die in shame<br>That, Talbot dead, great York might bear the name. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Lucy |
| CAPTAIN | Here is Sir William Lucy, who with me<br>Set from our o’ermatched forces forth for aid. |
| SOMERSET | How now, Sir William, whither were you sent? |
| LUCY | Whither, my lord? From bought and sold [[Lord Talbot]],<br>Who, ringed about with bold adversity,<br>Cries out for noble York and Somerset<br>To beat assailing [[Death\|death]] from his weak legions;<br>And whiles the honourable captain there<br>Drops bloody sweat from his war-wearied limbs<br>And, unadvantaged, ling’ring looks for rescue,<br>You his false hopes, the trust of England’s [[Honour\|honour]],<br>Keep off aloof with worthless emulation.<br>Let not your private discord keep away<br>The levied succours that should lend him aid,<br>While he, renowned noble gentleman,<br>Yield up his life unto a world of odds.<br>Orléans the Bastard, Charles, and Burgundy,<br>Alençon, René, compass him about,<br>And Talbot perisheth by your default. |
| SOMERSET | York set him on; York should have sent him aid. |
| LUCY | And York as fast upon your grace exclaims,<br>Swearing that you withhold his levied [[Horse\|horse]]<br>Collected for this expedition. |
| SOMERSET | York lies. He might have sent and had the [[Horse\|horse]].<br>I owe him little duty and less [[Love\|love]],<br>And take foul scorn to fawn on him by sending. |
| LUCY | The fraud of England, not the force of France,<br>Hath now entrapped the noble-minded Talbot.<br>Never to England shall he bear his life,<br>But dies betrayed to fortune by your strife. |
| SOMERSET | Come, go. I will dispatch the horsemen straight. |
| *(didascalia)* | Within six hours they will be at his aid. |
| LUCY | Too late comes rescue. He is ta’en or slain,<br>For fly he could not if he would have fled,<br>And fly would Talbot never, though he might. |
| SOMERSET | If he be dead, brave Talbot, then adieu. |
| LUCY | His fame lives in the world, his shame in you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 4, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Lord Talbot]] and his son John |
| TALBOT | O young John Talbot, I did send for thee<br>To tutor thee in stratagems of war,<br>That Talbot’s name might be in thee revived<br>When sapless age and weak unable limbs<br>Should bring thy father to his drooping chair.<br>But O—malignant and ill-boding [[Stars\|stars]]!—<br>Now thou art come unto a feast of [[Death\|death]],<br>A terrible and unavoided danger.<br>Therefore, dear boy, mount on my swiftest [[Horse\|horse]],<br>And I’ll direct thee how thou shalt escape<br>By sudden flight. Come, dally not, be gone. |
| JOHN | Is my name Talbot, and am I your son,<br>And shall I fly? O, if you [[Love\|love]] my mother,<br>Dishonour not her honourable name<br>To make a bastard and a slave of me.<br>The world will say he is not Talbot’s [[Blood\|blood]]<br>That basely fled when noble Talbot stood. |
| TALBOT | Fly to [[Revenge\|revenge]] my [[Death\|death]] if I be slain. |
| JOHN | He that flies so will ne’er return again. |
| TALBOT | If we both stay, we both are sure to die. |
| JOHN | Then let me stay and, father, do you fly.<br>Your loss is great; so your regard should be.<br>My worth unknown, no loss is known in me.<br>Upon my [[Death\|death]] the French can little boast;<br>In yours they will: in you all hopes are lost.<br>Flight cannot stain the [[Honour\|honour]] you have won,<br>But mine it will, that no exploit have done.<br>You fled for vantage, everyone will swear,<br>But if I bow, they’ll say it was for [[Fear\|fear]].<br>There is no [[Hope\|hope]] that ever I will stay<br>If the first hour I shrink and run away.<br>Here on my knee I beg [[Mortality\|mortality]]<br>Rather than life preserved with infamy. |
| TALBOT | Shall all thy mother’s hopes lie in one tomb? |
| JOHN | Ay, rather than I’ll shame my mother’s womb. |
| TALBOT | Upon my blessing I command thee go. |
| JOHN | To fight I will, but not to fly the foe. |
| TALBOT | Part of thy father may be saved in thee. |
| JOHN | No part of him but will be shamed in me. |
| TALBOT | Thou never hadst renown, nor canst not lose it. |
| JOHN | Yes, your renowned name—shall flight abuse it? |
| TALBOT | Thy father’s charge shall clear thee from that stain. |
| JOHN | You cannot witness for me, being slain.<br>If [[Death\|death]] be so apparent, then both fly. |
| TALBOT | And leave my followers here to fight and die?<br>My age was never tainted with such shame. |
| JOHN | And shall my youth be guilty of such blame?<br>No more can I be severed from your side<br>Than can yourself your [[Self\|self]] in twain divide.<br>Stay, go, do what you will: the like do I,<br>For live I will not if my father die. |
| TALBOT | Then here I take my leave of thee, fair son,<br>Born to eclipse thy life this afternoon.<br>Come, side by side together live and die,<br>And [[Soul\|soul]] with [[Soul\|soul]] from France to heaven fly. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Excursions, wherein [[Lord Talbot]]’s son John is hemmed about by French soldiers and Talbot rescues him. The English drive off the French |
| TALBOT | Saint George and victory! Fight, soldiers, fight!<br>The Regent hath with Talbot broke his word,<br>And left us to the rage of France his [[Sword\|sword]].<br>Where is John Talbot? (To John) Pause and take thy<br>breath.<br>I gave thee life, and rescued thee from [[Death\|death]]. |
| JOHN | O twice my father, twice am I thy son:<br>The life thou gav‘st me first was lost and done<br>Till with thy warlike [[Sword\|sword]], despite of fate,<br>To my determined [[Time\|time]] thou gav’st new date. |
| TALBOT | When from the [[Dauphin]]’s crest thy [[Sword\|sword]] struck [[Fire\|fire]]<br>It warmed thy father’s heart with proud [[Desire\|desire]]<br>Of bold-faced victory. Then leaden age,<br>Quickened with youthful spleen and warlike rage,<br>Beat down Alençon, Orléans, Burgundy,<br>And from the [[Pride\|pride]] of Gallia rescued thee.<br>The ireful Bastard Orléans, that drew [[Blood\|blood]]<br>From thee, my boy, and had the maidenhood<br>Of thy first fight, I soon encountered,<br>And interchanging blows, I quickly shed<br>Some of his bastard [[Blood\|blood]], and in disgrace<br>Bespoke him thus: ‘Contaminated, base,<br>And misbegotten [[Blood\|blood]] I spill of thine,<br>Mean and right poor, for that pure [[Blood\|blood]] of mine<br>Which thou didst force from Talbot, my brave boy.’<br>Here, purposing the Bastard to destroy,<br>Came in strong rescue. Speak thy father’s care:<br>Art thou not weary, John? How dost thou fare?<br>Wilt thou yet leave the battle, boy, and fly,<br>Now thou art sealed the son of chivalry?<br>Fly to [[Revenge\|revenge]] my [[Death\|death]] when I am dead;<br>The help of one stands me in little stead.<br>O, too much folly is it, [[Well\|well]] I wot,<br>To hazard all our lives in one small boat.<br>If I today die not with Frenchmen’s rage,<br>Tomorrow I shall die with mickle age.<br>By me they nothing gain, and if I stay<br>‘Tis but the short’ning of my life one day.<br>In thee thy mother dies, our household’s name,<br>My [[Death\|death]]’s [[Revenge\|revenge]], thy youth, and England’s fame.<br>All these and more we hazard by thy stay;<br>All these are saved if thou wilt fly away. |
| JOHN | The [[Sword\|sword]] of Orléans hath not made me smart;<br>These words of yours draw life-[[Blood\|blood]] from my heart.<br>On that advantage, bought with such a shame,<br>To save a paltry life and slay bright fame,<br>Before young Talbot from old Talbot fly<br>The coward [[Horse\|horse]] that bears me fall and die;<br>And like me to the peasant boys of France,<br>To be shame’s scorn and subject of mischance !<br>Surely, by all the glory you have won,<br>An if I fly I am not Talbot’s son.<br>Then talk no more of flight; it is no boot.<br>If son to Talbot, die at Talbot’s foot. |
| TALBOT | Then follow thou thy desp’rate sire of Crete,<br>Thou Icarus; thy life to me is sweet.<br>If thou wilt fight, fight by thy father’s side,<br>And commendable proved, let’s die in [[Pride\|pride]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Excursions. Enter old [[Lord Talbot]] led by a Servant |
| TALBOT | Where is my other life? Mine own is gone.<br>O where’s young Talbot, where is valiant John?<br>Triumphant [[Death\|death]] smeared with captivity,<br>Young Talbot’s valour makes me smile at thee.<br>When he perceived me shrink and on my knee,<br>His bloody [[Sword\|sword]] he brandished over me,<br>And like a hungry [[Lion\|lion]] did commence<br>Rough deeds of rage and stern impatience.<br>But when my angry guardant stood [[Alone\|alone]],<br>Tend‘ring my ruin and assailed of none,<br>Dizzy-eyed fury and great rage of heart<br>Suddenly made him from my side to start<br>Into the clust’ring battle of the French,<br>And in that sea of [[Blood\|blood]] my boy did drench<br>His over-mounting spirit; and there died<br>My Icarus, my blossom, in his [[Pride\|pride]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter English soldiers with John Talbot’s [[Body\|body]], borne |
| SERVANT | O my odear lord, lo where your son is borne. |
| TALBOT | Thou antic [[Death\|death]], which laugh‘st us here to scorn,<br>Anon from thy insulting tyranny,<br>Coupled in bonds of perpetuity,<br>Two Talbots winged through the lither sky<br>In thy despite shall scape [[Mortality\|mortality]].<br>(To John) O thou whose wounds become hard-favoured<br>[[Death\|death]],<br>Speak to thy father ere thou yield thy breath.<br>Brave [[Death\|death]] by speaking, whether he will or no;<br>Imagine him a Frenchman and thy foe.—<br>Poor boy, he smiles, methinks, as who should say<br>‘Had [[Death\|death]] been French, then [[Death\|death]] had died today’.<br>Come, come, and lay him in his father’s arms.<br>Soldiers lay John in Talbot’s arms<br>My spirit can no longer bear these harms.<br>Soldiers, adieu. I have what I would have,<br>Now my old arms are young John Talbot’s [[Grave\|grave]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | He dies. Alarum. Exeunt soldiers leaving the bodies |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]], the dukes of Alencon and Burgundy, the Bastard of Orléans, and [[Joan la Pucelle]] |
| CHARLES | Had York and Somerset brought rescue in,<br>We should have found a bloody day of this. |
| BASTARD | How the young whelp of Talbot’s, raging wood,<br>Did flesh his puny [[Sword\|sword]] in Frenchmen’s [[Blood\|blood]]! |
| JOAN | Once I encountered him, and thus I said:<br>‘Thou maiden youth, be vanquished by a maid.’<br>But with a proud, majestical high scorn<br>He answered thus: ‘Young Talbot was not born<br>To be the pillage of a giglot wench.’<br>So rushing in the bowels of the French,<br>He left me proudly, as unworthy fight. |
| BURGUNDY | Doubtless he would have made a noble knight.<br>See where he lies inhearsèd in the arms<br>Of the most bloody nurser of his harms. |
| BASTARD | Hew them to pieces, hack their bones asunder,<br>Whose life was England’s glory, Gallia’s [[Wonder\|wonder]]. |
| CHARLES | O no, forbear; for that which we have fled<br>During the life, let us not wrong it dead. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir William Lucy with a French herald |
| LUCY | Herald, conduct me to the [[Dauphin]]’s tent<br>To know who hath obtained the glory of the day. |
| CHARLES | On what submissive message art thou sent? |
| LUCY | Submission, [[Dauphin]]?‘Tis a mere French word.<br>We English warriors wot not what it means.<br>I come to know what prisoners thou hast ta’en,<br>And to survey the bodies of the dead. |
| CHARLES | For prisoners ask‘st thou ? Hell our [[Prison\|prison]] is.<br>But tell me whom thou seek’st. |
| LUCY | But where’s the great Alcides of the field,<br>Valiant [[Lord Talbot]], Earl of Shrewsbury,<br>Created for his rare [[success]] in arms<br>Great Earl of Wexford, Waterford, and Valence,<br>[[Lord Talbot]] of Goodrich and Urchinfield,<br>Lord Strange of Blackmere, Lord Verdun of Alton,<br>Lord Cromwell of Wingfield, Lord Furnival of Sheffield,<br>The thrice victorious lord of Falconbridge,<br>Knight of the noble order of Saint George,<br>Worthy Saint [[Michael]] and the Golden Fleece,<br>Great Maréchal to Henry the Sixth<br>Of all his wars within the realm of France? |
| JOAN | Here’s a silly, stately style indeed.<br>The Turk, that two-and-fifty kingdoms hath,<br>Writes not so tedious a style as this.<br>Him that thou magnifi’st with all these titles<br>Stinking and flyblown lies here at our feet. |
| LUCY | Is Talbot slain, the Frenchmen’s only scourge,<br>Your kingdom’s terror and black Nemesis?<br>O, were mine eye-balls into bullets turned,<br>That I in rage might shoot them at your faces!<br>O, that I could but call these dead to life!—<br>It were enough to fright the realm of France.<br>Were but his picture left amongst you here<br>It would amaze the proudest of you all.<br>Give me their bodies, that I may bear them hence<br>And give them burial as beseems their worth. |
| JOAN | (to Charles)<br>I think this upstart is old Talbot’s [[Ghost\|ghost]],<br>He speaks with such a proud commanding spirit.<br>For God’s sake let him have them. To keep them here<br>They would but stink and putrefy the air. |
| CHARLES | Go, take their bodies hence. |
| LUCY | I’ll bear them hence, but from their ashes shall be<br>reared<br>A phoenix that shall make all France afeard. |
| CHARLES | So we be rid of them, do with them what thou wilt. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Lucy and herald with the bodies |
| CHARLES | And now to [[Paris]] in this conquering vein.<br>All will be ours, now bloody Talbot’s slain. Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 5.1 Sennet. Enter [[King]] Henry, the Dukes of [[Gloucester]] and Exeter, and others |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (to [[Gloucester]])<br>Have you perused the letters from the Pope,<br>The Emperor, and the Earl of Armagnac? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | I have, my lord, and their intent is this:<br>They humbly sue unto your excellence<br>To have a godly peace concluded of<br>Between the realms of England and of France. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | How doth your grace affect their motion? |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | [[Well]], my good lord, and as the only means<br>To stop effusion of our Christian [[Blood\|blood]]<br>And ’stablish quietness on every side. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Ay, marry, uncle; for I always thought<br>It was both impious and unnatural<br>That such immanity and bloody strife<br>Should reign among professors of one [[Faith\|faith]]. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Beside, my lord, the sooner to effect<br>And surer bind this knot of amity,<br>The Earl of Armagnac, near knit to Charles—<br>A man of great authority in France—<br>Proffers his only daughter to your grace<br>In [[Marriage\|marriage]], with a large and sumptuous dowry. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | [[Marriage]], uncle? Alas, my years are young,<br>And fitter is my study and my books<br>Than wanton dalliance with a paramour. |
| *(didascalia)* | Yet call th’ambassadors, Exit one or more |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | and as you please,<br>So let them have their answers every one.<br>I shall be [[Well\|well]] content with any choice<br>Tends to God’s glory and my country’s weal. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Bishop of Winchester, now in cardinal’s habit, and three ambassadors, one a Papal Legate |
| EXETER | (aside)<br>What, is my lord of Winchester installed<br>And called unto a cardinal’s degree?<br>Then I perceive that will be verified<br>Henry the Fifth did sometime prophesy:<br>‘If once he come to be a cardinal,<br>He’ll make his cap co-equal with the [[Crown\|crown]].’ |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | My lords ambassadors, your several suits<br>Have been considered and debated on.<br>Your purpose is both good and reasonable,<br>And therefore are we certainly resolved<br>To draw conditions of a friendly peace,<br>Which by my lord of Winchester we mean<br>Shall be transported presently to France. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] to ambassadors |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And for the proffer of my lord your master,<br>I have informed his highness so at large<br>As, liking of the lady’s virtuous gifts,<br>Her [[Beauty\|beauty]], and the value of her dower,<br>He doth intend she shall be England’s [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[King\|KING]] HENRY to ambassadors |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | In argument and proof of which contract<br>Bear her this [[Jewel\|jewel]], pledge of my affection.<br>(To [[Gloucester]]) And so, my lord Protector, see them<br>guarded<br>And safely brought to Dover, wherein shipped,<br>Commit them to the fortune of the sea. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally all but Winchester and Legate |
| WINCHESTER | Stay, my lord legate; you shall first receive<br>The sum of money which I promised<br>Should be delivered to his holiness<br>For clothing me in these [[Grave\|grave]] ornaments. |
| *(didascalia)* | I will attend upon your lordship’s leisure. Exit |
| WINCHESTER | Now Winchester will not submit, I trow,<br>Or be inferior to the proudest peer.<br>Humphrey of [[Gloucester]], thou shalt [[Well\|well]] perceive<br>That nor in birth or for authority<br>The Bishop will be overborne by thee.<br>I’ll either make thee stoop and bend thy knee, |
| *(didascalia)* | Or sack this country with a mutiny. Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]] reading a letter, the Dukes of Burgundy and Alençon, the Bastard of Orléans, René Duke of Anjou, and oan la Pucelle |
| CHARLES | These news, my lords, may cheer our drooping spirits.<br>‘Tis said the stout Parisians do revolt<br>And turn again unto the warlike French.<br>ALENÇON<br>Then march to [[Paris]], royal Charles of France,<br>And keep not back your powers in dalliance. |
| JOAN | Peace be amongst them if they turn to us;<br>Else, ruin combat with their palaces! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a Scout |
| SCOUT | [[success\|Success]] unto our valiant general,<br>And happiness to his accomplices. |
| CHARLES | What tidings send our scouts? I prithee speak. |
| SCOUT | The English army, that divided was<br>Into two parties, is now conjoined in one,<br>And means to give you battle presently. |
| CHARLES | Somewhat too sudden, sirs, the warning is;<br>But we will presently provide for them. |
| BURGUNDY | I trust the [[Ghost\|ghost]] of Talbot is not there. |
| *(didascalia)* | JOAN |
| BURGUNDY | Now he is gone, my lord, you need not [[Fear\|fear]].<br>Of all base passions, [[Fear\|fear]] is most accursed.<br>Command the conquest, Charles, it shall be thine;<br>Let Henry fret and all the world repine. |
| CHARLES | Then on, my lords; and France be fortunate! Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Excursions. Enter [[Joan la Pucelle]] |
| JOAN | The Regent conquers, and the Frenchmen fly.<br>Now help, ye charming spells and periapts,<br>And ye choice spirits that admonish me<br>And give me signs of future accidents. |
| *(didascalia)* | Thunder |
| JOAN | You speedy helpers, that are substitutes<br>Under the lordly monarch of the north,<br>Appear, and aid me in this enterprise. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Fiends |
| JOAN | This [[Speed\|speed]] and quick appearance argues proof<br>Of your accustomed diligence to me.<br>Now, ye familiar spirits that are culled<br>Out of the powerful regions under earth,<br>Help me this once, that France may get the field.<br>They walk and speak not<br>O, hold me not with [[Silence\|silence]] overlong!<br>Where I was wont to feed you with my [[Blood\|blood]],<br>I’ll lop a member off and give it you<br>In earnest of a further benefit,<br>So you do condescend to help me now.<br>They hang their heads<br>No [[Hope\|hope]] to have redress? My [[Body\|body]] shall<br>Pay recompense if you will grant my suit.<br>They shake their heads<br>Cannot my [[Body\|body]] nor [[Blood\|blood]]-sacrifice<br>Entreat you to your wonted furtherance?<br>Then take my [[Soul\|soul]]—my [[Body\|body]], [[Soul\|soul]], and all—<br>Before that England give the French the foil.<br>They depart<br>See, they forsake me. Now the [[Time\|time]] is come<br>That France must vail her lofty-plumed crest<br>And let her head fall into England’s lap.<br>My ancient incantations are too weak,<br>And hell too strong for me to buckle with.<br>Now, France, thy glory droopeth to the [[Dust\|dust]]. Exit |

### Atto 5, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Excursions. The Dukes of Burgundy and York fight hand to hand. The French fly. [[Joan la Pucelle]] is taken |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | [[Damsel]] of France, I think I have you fast.<br>Unchain your spirits now with spelling charms,<br>And try if they can gain your liberty.<br>A goodly prize, fit for the devil’s grace! |
| *(didascalia)* | To his soldiers See how the ugly [[Witch\|witch]] doth bend her |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | brows, 5<br>As if with Circe she would change my shape. |
| JOAN | Changed to a worser shape thou canst not be. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | O, [[Charles the Dauphin]] is a proper man.<br>No shape but his can please your dainty eye. |
| JOAN | A plaguing mischief light on Charles and thee,<br>And may ye both be suddenly surprised<br>By bloody hands in sleeping on your beds! |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Fell banning hag, enchantress, hold thy tongue. |
| JOAN | I prithee give me leave to curse awhile. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Curse, miscreant, when thou comest to the stake. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Alarum. Enter the [[Earl of Suffolk]] with Margaret in his hand |
| SUFFOLK | Be what thou wilt, thou art my prisoner.<br>He gazes on her<br>O fairest [[Beauty\|beauty]], do not [[Fear\|fear]] nor fly,<br>For I will touch thee but with reverent hands,<br>And lay them gently on thy tender side.<br>I kiss these fingers for eternal peace.<br>Who art thou? Say, that I may [[Honour\|honour]] thee. |
| MARGARET | Margaret my name, and daughter to a [[King\|king]],<br>The [[King]] of Naples, whosoe’er thou art. |
| SUFFOLK | An earl I am, and Suffolk am I called.<br>Be not offended, [[Nature\|nature]]’s miracle,<br>Thou art allotted to be ta’en by me.<br>So doth the swan his downy cygnets save,<br>Keeping them prisoner underneath his wings.<br>Yet if this servile usage once offend,<br>Go, and be free again, as Suffolk’s friend.<br>She is going<br>O stay! (Aside) I have no power to let her pass.<br>My hand would free her, but my heart says no.<br>As plays the sun upon the glassy stream,<br>Twinkling another counterfeited beam,<br>So seems this gorgeous [[Beauty\|beauty]] to mine eyes.<br>Fain would I woo her, yet I dare not speak.<br>I’ll call for pen and ink, and write my mind.<br>Fie, de la Pole, disable not thyself!<br>Hast not a tongue? Is she not here to hear?<br>Wilt thou be daunted at a woman’s sight?<br>Ay, [[Beauty\|beauty]]’s princely majesty is such<br>Confounds the tongue, and makes the senses rough. |
| MARGARET | Say, [[Earl of Suffolk]]—if thy name be so—<br>What ransom must I pay before I pass?<br>For I perceive I am thy prisoner. |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>How canst thou tell she will deny thy suit<br>Before thou make a trial of her [[Love\|love]]? |
| MARGARET | Why speak’st thou not? What ransom must I pay? |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>She’s beautiful, and therefore to be wooed;<br>She is a woman, therefore to be won. |
| MARGARET | Wilt thou accept of ransom, yea or no? |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>Fond man, remember that thou hast a wife;<br>Then how can Margaret be thy paramour? |
| MARGARET | (aside)<br>I were best to leave him, for he will not hear. |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>There all is marred; there lies a cooling card. |
| MARGARET | (aside)<br>He talks at random; sure the man is mad. |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>And yet a dispensation may be had. |
| MARGARET | And yet I would that you would answer me. |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>I’ll win this Lady Margaret. For whom?<br>Why, for my [[King\|king]]—tush, that’s a wooden thing. |
| MARGARET | (aside)<br>He talks of wood. It is some carpenter. |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>Yet so my fancy may be satisfied,<br>And peace established between these realms.<br>But there remains a scruple in that too,<br>For though her father be the [[King]] of Naples,<br>Duke of Anjou and Maine, yet is he poor,<br>And our nobility will scorn the match. |
| MARGARET | Hear ye, captain? Are you not at leisure? |
| SUFFOLK | (aside)<br>It shall be so, disdain they ne’er so much.<br>Henry is youthful, and will quickly yield.<br>(To Margaret) Madam, I have a secret to reveal. |
| MARGARET | (aside)<br>What though I be enthralled, he seems a knight<br>And will not any way dishonour me. |
| SUFFOLK | Lady, vouchsafe to listen what I say. |
| MARGARET | (aside)<br>Perhaps I shall be rescued by the French,<br>And then I need not crave his courtesy. |
| SUFFOLK | Sweet madam, give me hearing in a cause. |
| MARGARET | (aside)<br>Tush, women have been captivate ere now. |
| SUFFOLK | Lady, wherefore talk you so? |
| MARGARET | I cry you [[Mercy\|mercy]], ’tis but quid for quo. |
| SUFFOLK | Say, gentle Princess, would you not suppose<br>Your bondage happy to be made a [[Queen\|queen]]? |
| MARGARET | To be a [[Queen\|queen]] in bondage is more vile<br>Than is a slave in base servility,<br>For princes should be free. |
| SUFFOLK | And so shall you,<br>If happy England’s royal [[King\|king]] be free. |
| MARGARET | Why, what concerns his [[Freedom\|freedom]] unto me? |
| SUFFOLK | I’ll undertake to make thee Henry’s [[Queen\|queen]],<br>To put a golden sceptre in thy hand,<br>And set a precious [[Crown\|crown]] upon thy head,<br>If thou wilt condescend to be my— |
| MARGARET | What? |
| SUFFOLK | His [[Love\|love]]. |
| MARGARET | I am unworthy to be Henry’s wife. |
| SUFFOLK | No, gentle madam, I unworthy am<br>To woo so fair a dame to be his wife<br>(Aside) And have no portion in the choice myself.—<br>How say you, madam; are ye so content? |
| MARGARET | An if my father please, I am content. |
| SUFFOLK | Then call our captains and our colours forth, |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter captains, colours, and trumpeters |
| SUFFOLK | And, madam, at your father’s [[Castle\|castle]] walls<br>We’ll crave a parley to confer with him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound a parley. Enter René Duke of Anjou on the walls |
| SUFFOLK | See, Rene, see thy daughter prisoner.<br>RENÉ<br>To whom ? |
| SUFFOLK | To me.<br>RENÉ Suffolk, what remedy?<br>I am a soldier, and unapt to weep<br>Or to exclaim on fortune’s fickleness. |
| SUFFOLK | Yes, there is remedy enough, my lord.<br>Assent, and for thy [[Honour\|honour]] give consent<br>Thy daughter shall be wedded to my [[King\|king]],<br>Whom I with [[Pain\|pain]] have wooed and won thereto;<br>And this her easy-held [[Imprisonment\|imprisonment]]<br>Hath gained thy daughter princely liberty.<br>RENÉ<br>Speaks Suffolk as he thinks? |
| SUFFOLK | Fair Margaret knows<br>That Suffolk doth not flatter, face or feign.<br>RENÉ<br>Upon thy princely warrant I descend<br>To give thee answer of thy just demand. |
| *(didascalia)* | And here I will expect thy coming. Exit Rene above |
| *(didascalia)* | Trumpets sound. Enter René |
| RENE | Welcome, brave Earl, into our territories.<br>Command in Anjou what your [[Honour\|honour]] pleases. |
| SUFFOLK | Thanks, René, happy for so sweet a [[Child\|child]],<br>Fit to be made companion with a [[King\|king]].<br>What answer makes your grace unto my suit?<br>RENÉ<br>Since thou dost deign to woo her little worth<br>To be the princely bride of such a lord,<br>Upon condition I may quietly<br>Enjoy mine own, the countries Maine and Anjou,<br>Free from oppression or the stroke of war,<br>My daughter shall be Henry’s, if he please. |
| SUFFOLK | That is her ransom. I deliver her,<br>And those two counties I will undertake<br>Your grace shall [[Well\|well]] and quietly enjoy.<br>RENÉ<br>And I again in Henry’s royal name,<br>As deputy unto that gracious [[King\|king]],<br>Give thee her hand for sign of plighted [[Faith\|faith]]. |
| SUFFOLK | René of France, I give thee kingly thanks,<br>Because this is in traffic of a [[King\|king]].<br>(Aside) And yet methinks I could be [[Well\|well]] content<br>To be mine own attorney in this case.<br>(To René) I’ll over then to England with this news,<br>And make this [[Marriage\|marriage]] to be solemnized.<br>So farewell, René; set this diamond safe<br>In golden palaces, as it becomes.<br>RENÉ<br>I do embrace thee as I would embrace<br>The Christian prince [[King]] Henry, were he here. |
| MARGARET | (to Suffolk)<br>Farewell, my lord. Good wishes, praise, and prayers<br>Shall Suffolk ever have of Margaret.<br>She is going |
| SUFFOLK | Farewell, sweet madam; but hark you, Margaret—<br>No princely commendations to my [[King\|king]]? |
| MARGARET | Such commendations as becomes a maid,<br>A virgin, and his servant, say to him. |
| SUFFOLK | Words sweetly placed, and modestly directed. |
| *(didascalia)* | She is going |
| SUFFOLK | But madam, I must trouble you again—<br>No loving token to his majesty? |
| MARGARET | Yes, my good lord : a pure unspotted heart,<br>Never yet taint with [[Love\|love]], I send the [[King]]. |
| SUFFOLK | And this withal.<br>He kisses her |
| MARGARET | That for thyself; I will not so presume<br>To send such peevish tokens to a [[King\|king]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt René and Margaret |
| *(didascalia)* | SUFFOLK aside |
| MARGARET | O, wert thou for myself!—but Suffolk, stay.<br>Thou mayst not wander in that labyrinth.<br>There Minotaurs and ugly treasons lurk.<br>Solicit Henry with her wondrous praise.<br>Bethink thee on her virtues that surmount,<br>Mad natural graces that extinguish art.<br>Repeat their semblance often on the seas,<br>That when thou com’st to kneel at Henry’s feet<br>Thou mayst bereave him of his wits with [[Wonder\|wonder]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Richard]] [[Duke of York]], the [[Earl of Warwick]], and a Shepherd |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Bring forth that sorceress condemned to burn. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Joan la Pucelle]] guarded |
| SHEPHERD | Ah, Joan, this kills thy father’s heart outright.<br>Have I sought every country far and near,<br>And now it is my chance to find thee out<br>Must I behold thy timeless cruel [[Death\|death]]?<br>Ah Joan, sweet daughter Joan, I’ll die with thee. |
| JOAN | Decrepit miser, base ignoble wretch,<br>I am descended of a gentler [[Blood\|blood]].<br>Thou art no father nor no friend of mine. |
| SHEPHERD | Out, out!—My lords, an’t please you, ‘tis not so.<br>I did beget her, all the parish knows.<br>Her mother liveth yet, can testify<br>She was the first fruit of my bach’lorship. |
| WARWICK | (to Joan)<br>Graceless, wilt thou deny thy parentage? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | This argues what her kind of life hath been—<br>Wicked and vile; and so her [[Death\|death]] concludes. |
| SHEPHERD | Fie, Joan, that thou wilt be so obstacle.<br>God knows thou art a collop of my flesh,<br>And for thy sake have I shed many a tear.<br>Deny me not, I prithee, gentle Joan. |
| JOAN | Peasant, avaunt! (To the English) You have suborned<br>this man<br>Of purpose to obscure my noble birth. |
| SHEPHERD | (to the English)<br>‘Tis true I gave a noble to the priest<br>The morn that I was wedded to her mother.<br>(To Joan) Kneel down, and take my blessing, good my<br>girl.<br>Wilt thou not stoop? Now cursed be the [[Time\|time]]<br>Of thy nativity. I would the milk<br>Thy mother gave thee when thou sucked’st her breast<br>Had been a little ratsbane for thy sake.<br>Or else, when thou didst keep my lambs afield,<br>I wish some ravenous wolf had eaten thee.<br>Dost thou deny thy father, cursed drab?<br>(To the English) O burn her, burn her! Hanging is too<br>good. Exit |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | (to guards)<br>Take her away, for she hath lived too long,<br>To fill the world with vicious qualities. |
| JOAN | First let me tell you whom you have condemned:<br>Not one begotten of a shepherd swain,<br>But issued from the progeny of kings;<br>Virtuous and holy, chosen from above<br>By inspiration of celestial grace<br>To work exceeding miracles on earth.<br>I never had to do with wicked spirits;<br>But you that are polluted with your lusts,<br>Stained with the guiltless [[Blood\|blood]] of innocents,<br>Corrupt and tainted with a thousand vices—<br>Because you want the grace that others have,<br>You judge it straight a thing impossible<br>To compass wonders but by help of devils.<br>No, misconceived Joan of Arc hath been<br>A virgin from her tender infancy,<br>Chaste and immaculate in very thought,<br>Whose maiden-[[Blood\|blood]] thus rigorously effused<br>Will cry for vengeance at the gates of heaven. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Ay, ay, (to guards) away with her to execution. |
| WARWICK | (to guards)<br>And hark ye, sirs: because she is a maid,<br>Spare for no faggots. Let there be enough.<br>Place barrels of pitch upon the fatal stake,<br>That so her torture may be shortened. |
| JOAN | Will nothing turn your unrelenting hearts?<br>Then Joan, discover thine infirmity,<br>That warranteth by law to be thy privilege:<br>I am with [[Child\|child]], ye bloody homicides.<br>Murder not then the fruit within my womb,<br>Although ye hale me to a violent [[Death\|death]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Now heaven forfend—the holy maid with [[Child\|child]]? |
| WARWICK | (to Joan)<br>The greatest miracle that e’er ye wrought.<br>Is all your strict preciseness come to this? |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | She and the [[Dauphin]] have been ingling.<br>I did imagine what would be her refuge. |
| WARWICK | [[Well]], go to, we will have no bastards live,<br>Especially since Charles must father it. |
| JOAN | You are deceived. My [[Child\|child]] is none of his.<br>It was Alençon that enjoyed my [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Alençon, that notorious Machiavel?<br>It dies an if it had a thousand lives. |
| JOAN | O give me leave, I have deluded you.<br>‘Twas neither Charles nor yet the Duke I named,<br>But René [[King]] of Naples that prevailed. |
| WARWICK | A married man?—That’s most intolerable. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Why, here’s a girl; I think she knows not [[Well\|well]]—<br>There were so many—whom she may accuse. |
| WARWICK | It’s sign she hath been liberal and free. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | And yet forsooth she is a virgin pure!<br>(To joan) Strumpet, thy words condemn thy brat and<br>thee.<br>Use no entreaty, for it is in vain. |
| JOAN | Then lead me hence; with whom I leave my curse.<br>May never glorious sun reflex his beams<br>Upon the country where you make abode,<br>But darkness and the gloomy shade of [[Death\|death]]<br>Environ you till mischief and [[Despair\|despair]]<br>Drive you to break your necks or hang yourselves. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Bishop of Winchester, now Cardinal |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | (to Joan)<br>Break thou in pieces, and consume to ashes,<br>Thou foul accursed minister of hell. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Joan, guarded |
| WINCHESTER | Lord Regent, I do greet your excellence<br>With letters of commission from the [[King]].<br>For know, my lords, the states of Christendom,<br>Moved with remorse of these outrageous broils,<br>Have earnestly implored a general peace<br>Betwixt our nation and the aspiring French,<br>And here at hand the [[Dauphin]] and his train<br>Approacheth to confer about some matter. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Is all our travail turned to this effect?<br>After the slaughter of so many peers,<br>So many captains, gentlemen, and soldiers<br>That in this quarrel have been overthrown<br>And sold their bodies for their country’s benefit,<br>Shall we at last conclude effeminate peace?<br>Have we not lost most part of all the towns<br>By treason, falsehood, and by treachery,<br>Our great progenitors had conquered?<br>O Warwick, Warwick, I foresee with grief<br>The utter loss of all the realm of France! |
| WARWICK | Be patient, York. If we conclude a peace<br>It shall be with such strict and severe covenants<br>As little shall the Frenchmen gain thereby. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Charles the Dauphin]], the Duke of Alençon, the Bastard of Orléans, and René Duke of Anjou |
| CHARLES | Since, lords of England, it is thus agreed<br>That peaceful truce shall be proclaimed in France,<br>We come to be informed by yourselves<br>What the conditions of that league must be. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Speak, Winchester; for boiling choler chokes<br>The hollow passage of my poisoned voice<br>By sight of these our baleful enemies. |
| WINCHESTER | Charles and the rest, it is enacted thus:<br>That, in regard [[King]] Henry gives consent,<br>Of mere compassion and of lenity,<br>To ease your country of distressful war<br>And suffer you to breathe in fruitful peace,<br>You shall become true liegemen to his [[Crown\|crown]].<br>And, Charles, upon condition thou wilt swear<br>To pay him tribute and submit thyself,<br>Thou shalt be placed as viceroy under him,<br>And still enjoy thy regal dignity.<br>ALENÇON<br>Must he be then as [[Shadow\|shadow]] of himself?—<br>Adorn his temples with a coronet,<br>And yet in substance and authority<br>Retain but privilege of a private man?<br>This proffer is absurd and reasonless. |
| CHARLES | ’Tis known already that I am possessed<br>With more than half the Gallian territories,<br>And therein reverenced for their lawful [[King\|king]].<br>Shall I, for lucre of the rest unvanquished,<br>Detract so much from that prerogative<br>As to be called but viceroy of the whole?<br>No, lord ambassador, I’ll rather keep<br>That which I have than, coveting for more,<br>Be cast from possibility of all. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Insulting Charles, hast thou by secret means<br>Used intercession to obtain a league<br>And, now the matter grows to compromise,<br>Stand‘st thou aloof upon comparison?<br>Either accept the title thou usurp’st,<br>Of benefit proceeding from our [[King\|king]]<br>And not of any challenge of [[Desert\|desert]],<br>Or we will plague thee with incessant wars.<br>RENÉ (aside to Charles)<br>My lord, you do not [[Well\|well]] in obstinacy<br>To cavil in the course of this contract.<br>If once it be neglected, ten to one<br>We shall not find like opportunity.<br>ALENÇON (aside to Charles)<br>To say the [[Truth\|truth]], it is your policy<br>To save your subjects from such massacre<br>And ruthless slaughters as are daily seen<br>By our proceeding in hostility;<br>And therefore take this compact of a truce,<br>Although you break it when your pleasure serves. |
| WARWICK | How sayst thou, Charles? Shall our condition stand? |
| CHARLES | It shall,<br>Only reserved you claim no interest<br>In any of our towns of garrison. |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | Then swear allegiance to his majesty,<br>As thou art knight, never to disobey<br>Nor be rebellious to the [[Crown\|crown]] of England,<br>Thou nor thy nobles, to the [[Crown\|crown]] of England. |
| *(didascalia)* | They swear |
| [[Richard\|RICHARD]] [[Duke of York\|DUKE OF YORK]] | So, now dismiss your army when ye please.<br>Hang up your ensigns, let your drums be still;<br>For here we entertain a solemn peace. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Earl of Suffolk]], in conference with [[King]] Henry, and the Dukes of [[Gloucester]] and Exeter |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | (to Suffolk)<br>Your wondrous rare description, noble Earl,<br>Of beauteous Margaret hath astonished me.<br>Her virtues gracèd with external gifts<br>Do breed [[Love\|love]]’s settled passions in my heart,<br>And like as rigour of tempestuous gusts<br>Provokes the mightiest hulk against the tide,<br>So am I driven by breath of her renown<br>Either to suffer shipwreck or arrive<br>Where I may have fruition of her [[Love\|love]]. |
| SUFFOLK | Tush, my good lord, this superficial tale<br>Is but a preface of her worthy praise.<br>The chief perfections of that lovely dame,<br>Had I sufficient skill to utter them,<br>Would make a volume of enticing lines<br>Able to ravish any dull conceit;<br>And, which is more, she is not so divine,<br>So full replete with choice of all delights,<br>But with as humble lowliness of mind<br>She is content to be at your command—<br>Command, I mean, of virtuous chaste intents,<br>To [[Love\|love]] and [[Honour\|honour]] Henry as her lord. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | And otherwise will Henry ne‘er presume.<br>(To [[Gloucester]]) Therefore, my lord Protector, give<br>consent<br>That Marg’ret may be England’s royal [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | So should I give consent to flatter sin.<br>You know, my lord, your highness is betrothed<br>Unto another lady of esteem.<br>How shall we then dispense with that contract<br>And not deface your [[Honour\|honour]] with reproach? |
| SUFFOLK | As doth a ruler with unlawful oaths,<br>Or one that, at a triumph having vowed<br>To try his strength, forsaketh yet the lists<br>By reason of his adversary’s odds.<br>A poor earl’s daughter is unequal odds,<br>And therefore may be broke without offence. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Why, what, I pray, is Margaret more than that?<br>Her father is no better than an earl,<br>Although in glorious titles he excel. |
| SUFFOLK | Yes, my lord; her father is a [[King\|king]],<br>The [[King]] of Naples and Jerusalem,<br>And of such great authority in France<br>As his alliance will confirm our peace<br>And keep the Frenchmen in allegiance. |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | And so the Earl of Armagnac may do,<br>Because he is near kinsman unto Charles. |
| EXETER | Beside, his wealth doth warrant a liberal dower,<br>Where René sooner will receive than give. |
| SUFFOLK | A dower, my lords? Disgrace not so your [[King]]<br>That he should be so abject, base, and poor<br>To choose for wealth and not for perfect [[Love\|love]].<br>Henry is able to enrich his [[Queen\|queen]],<br>And not to seek a [[Queen\|queen]] to make him rich.<br>So worthless peasants bargain for their wives,<br>As market men for oxen, sheep, or [[Horse\|horse]].<br>[[Marriage]] is a matter of more worth<br>Than to be dealt in by attorneyship.<br>Not whom we will but whom his grace affects<br>Must be companion of his nuptial bed.<br>And therefore, lords, since he affects her most,<br>That most of all these reasons bindeth us:<br>In our opinions she should be preferred.<br>For what is wedlock forced but a hell,<br>An age of discord and continual strife,<br>Whereas the contrary bringeth bliss,<br>And is a pattern of celestial peace.<br>Whom should we match with Henry, being a [[King\|king]],<br>But Margaret, that is daughter to a [[King\|king]]?<br>Her peerless feature joined with her birth<br>Approves her fit for none but for a [[King\|king]].<br>Her valiant [[Courage\|courage]] and undaunted spirit,<br>More than in women commonly is seen,<br>Will answer our [[Hope\|hope]] in issue of a [[King\|king]].<br>For Henry, son unto a conqueror,<br>Is likely to beget more conquerors<br>If with a lady of so high resolve<br>As is fair Margaret he be linked in [[Love\|love]].<br>Then yield, my lords, and here conclude with me:<br>That Margaret shall be [[Queen\|queen]], and none but she. |
| [[King\|KING]] HENRY | Whether it be through force of your report,<br>My noble lord of Suffolk, or for that<br>My tender youth was never yet attaint<br>With any passion of inflaming [[Love\|love]],<br>I cannot tell; but this I am assured:<br>I feel such sharp dissension in my breast,<br>Such fierce alarums both of [[Hope\|hope]] and [[Fear\|fear]],<br>As I am sick with working of my thoughts.<br>Take therefore shipping; post, my lord, to France;<br>Agree to any covenants, and procure<br>That Lady Margaret do vouchsafe to come<br>To [[Cross\|cross]] the seas to England and be crowned<br>[[King]] Henry’s faithful and anointed [[Queen\|queen]].<br>For your expenses and sufficient charge,<br>Among the people gather up a tenth.<br>Be gone, I say; for till you do return<br>I rest perplexed with a thousand cares.<br>(To [[Gloucester]]) And you, good uncle, banish all offence.<br>If you do censure me by what you were,<br>Not what you are, I know it will excuse<br>This sudden execution of my will.<br>And so conduct me where from company<br>I may revolve and ruminate my grief. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with Exeter |
| [[Gloucester\|GLOUCESTER]] | Ay, grief, I [[Fear\|fear]] me, both at first and last. Exit |
